# Daily Summary: 2025-09-12

### LLM Ensemble for RAG: Role of Context Length in Zero-Shot Question Answering for BioASQ Challenge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08596v1)
- **Authors**: Dima Galat, Diego Molla-Aliod
- **Abstract**: Biomedical question answering (QA) poses significant challenges due to the need for precise interpretation of specialized knowledge drawn from a vast, complex, and rapidly evolving corpus. In this work, we explore how large language models (LLMs) can be used for information retrieval (IR), and an ensemble of zero-shot models can accomplish state-of-the-art performance on a domain-specific Yes/No QA task. Evaluating our approach on the BioASQ challenge tasks, we show that ensembles can outperform individual LLMs and in some cases rival or surpass domain-tuned systems - all while preserving generalizability and avoiding the need for costly fine-tuning or labeled data. Our method aggregates outputs from multiple LLM variants, including models from Anthropic and Google, to synthesize more accurate and robust answers. Moreover, our investigation highlights a relationship between context length and performance: while expanded contexts are meant to provide valuable evidence, they simultaneously risk information dilution and model disorientation. These findings emphasize IR as a critical foundation in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) approaches for biomedical QA systems. Precise, focused retrieval remains essential for ensuring LLMs operate within relevant information boundaries when generating answers from retrieved documents. Our results establish that ensemble-based zero-shot approaches, when paired with effective RAG pipelines, constitute a practical and scalable alternative to domain-tuned systems for biomedical question answering.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper:

This paper explores the use of large language models (LLMs) for biomedical question answering (QA), specifically focusing on a Yes/No task. It demonstrates that an ensemble of zero-shot LLMs, coupled with effective information retrieval (IR) in a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline, can achieve state-of-the-art performance on the BioASQ challenge, rivaling or surpassing domain-tuned systems without requiring costly fine-tuning or labeled data. The paper also highlights the importance of precise IR in RAG, noting the potential for context length to dilute relevant information. The results suggest that ensemble-based zero-shot approaches, combined with good IR, offer a practical and scalable alternative to domain-tuned LLMs in biomedical QA.

Now, for a rigorous and critical evaluation:

**Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies primarily in the *combination* of existing techniques, rather than the introduction of radically new methods. Using LLMs for QA, ensemble methods, and RAG pipelines are all well-established concepts. The specific *application* to biomedical QA, leveraging zero-shot capabilities and focusing on a Yes/No task within the BioASQ benchmark, provides some novelty. The specific findings around the impact of context length and the importance of precise IR contribute further incremental novelty. The paper also provides a useful comparison of zero-shot performance compared to domain-tuned systems in biomedical QA.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its demonstration of the effectiveness of zero-shot LLMs and ensemble methods in a challenging domain like biomedical QA. It shows that a relatively simple approach, leveraging readily available LLMs and focusing on good IR, can achieve impressive results. This has practical implications, as it suggests that significant investment in fine-tuning specific LLMs for biomedical tasks may not always be necessary. The finding about the potential drawbacks of excessively long context lengths is also valuable for practitioners building RAG pipelines. The paper contributes to the growing body of evidence supporting the power of RAG architectures.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and concise presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.
*   **Practical focus:** The research addresses a real-world problem in biomedical QA and offers practical solutions.
*   **Empirical validation:** The findings are supported by experiments on the BioASQ benchmark.
*   **Relevance:** The research aligns with current trends in LLM research, particularly in the area of RAG.
*   **Insightful observations:** The observation about the effect of context length provides valuable insights for practitioners.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited novelty:** As mentioned earlier, the methods used are largely based on existing techniques.
*   **Lack of in-depth analysis:** While the paper presents results, it could benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* the ensemble method is effective, and *how* different LLMs contribute to the overall performance. A more fine-grained error analysis would be valuable.
*   **Limited scope:** Focusing solely on Yes/No QA simplifies the problem. It is not clear how well the approach would generalize to more complex QA tasks.
*   **Dependency on specific LLMs:** The performance of the ensemble is likely dependent on the specific LLMs used. The availability and performance of these LLMs may change over time. This limits the long-term reproducibility and applicability of the findings.
*   **Limited comparison to recent advanced models:** While the paper compares to domain-tuned systems, it doesn't deeply analyze the performance differences relative to the newest general purpose models that have advanced capabilities out-of-the-box on complex tasks.

**Justification for the score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a useful application of existing techniques to a relevant problem. While the approach is not revolutionary, it demonstrates the potential of zero-shot LLMs and ensemble methods in biomedical QA and provides valuable insights into the importance of precise IR. However, the limited novelty, lack of in-depth analysis, and reliance on specific LLMs prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Memorization in Large Language Models in Medicine: Prevalence, Characteristics, and Implications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08604v1)
- **Authors**: Anran Li, Lingfei Qian, Mengmeng Du, Yu Yin, Yan Hu, Zihao Sun, Yihang Fu, Erica Stutz, Xuguang Ai, Qianqian Xie, Rui Zhu, Jimin Huang, Yifan Yang, Siru Liu, Yih-Chung Tham, Lucila Ohno-Machado, Hyunghoon Cho, Zhiyong Lu, Hua Xu, Qingyu Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated significant potential in medicine. To date, LLMs have been widely applied to tasks such as diagnostic assistance, medical question answering, and clinical information synthesis. However, a key open question remains: to what extent do LLMs memorize medical training data. In this study, we present the first comprehensive evaluation of memorization of LLMs in medicine, assessing its prevalence (how frequently it occurs), characteristics (what is memorized), volume (how much content is memorized), and potential downstream impacts (how memorization may affect medical applications). We systematically analyze common adaptation scenarios: (1) continued pretraining on medical corpora, (2) fine-tuning on standard medical benchmarks, and (3) fine-tuning on real-world clinical data, including over 13,000 unique inpatient records from Yale New Haven Health System. The results demonstrate that memorization is prevalent across all adaptation scenarios and significantly higher than reported in the general domain. Memorization affects both the development and adoption of LLMs in medicine and can be categorized into three types: beneficial (e.g., accurate recall of clinical guidelines and biomedical references), uninformative (e.g., repeated disclaimers or templated medical document language), and harmful (e.g., regeneration of dataset-specific or sensitive clinical content). Based on these findings, we offer practical recommendations to facilitate beneficial memorization that enhances domain-specific reasoning and factual accuracy, minimize uninformative memorization to promote deeper learning beyond surface-level patterns, and mitigate harmful memorization to prevent the leakage of sensitive or identifiable patient information.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of memorization in large language models (LLMs) adapted for medical applications. The authors investigated three common adaptation scenarios: continued pretraining on medical data, fine-tuning on medical benchmarks, and fine-tuning on real-world clinical data. They found that memorization is prevalent across all scenarios, even more so than in general-domain LLMs. They categorized memorization into beneficial, uninformative, and harmful types, and offered recommendations to promote beneficial memorization while mitigating the negative impacts of uninformative and harmful memorization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This paper tackles a crucial, yet under-explored, issue in the application of LLMs to medicine: memorization. While the benefits of LLMs in healthcare are often touted, the potential for memorization of training data poses significant risks, including privacy breaches, inaccurate recall of outdated information, and the hindering of genuine reasoning capabilities.

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in its *systematic and comprehensive* evaluation of memorization in medical LLMs. While memorization in general-domain LLMs has been studied, this paper provides the first in-depth analysis within the medical context. It's novel in its methodology: using real-world clinical data from a large hospital system (Yale New Haven Health System) to assess memorization under different adaptation scenarios. The categorization of memorization (beneficial, uninformative, harmful) is also a valuable contribution, providing a framework for understanding the different types of memorization and their respective implications. Furthermore, the suggested recommendations for mitigating harmful memorization and fostering beneficial recall contribute significantly to the practical application of this research.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work stems from its potential to directly impact the development and deployment of medical LLMs. By demonstrating the prevalence of memorization, particularly harmful memorization involving patient data, the paper highlights the urgent need for robust privacy safeguards and mitigation strategies. The classification scheme and the recommendations offer practical guidance to researchers and developers to ensure that LLMs are used responsibly and ethically in healthcare. The findings could also influence regulatory policies regarding the use of AI in medicine. This directly addresses current challenges in ethical AI development within healthcare.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive scope:** The study analyzes multiple adaptation scenarios, providing a holistic view of memorization in medical LLMs.
*   **Real-world data:** The use of clinical data from Yale New Haven Health System adds significant weight to the findings, making them directly relevant to real-world applications.
*   **Clear categorization:** The categorization of memorization into beneficial, uninformative, and harmful types simplifies the complex issue and allows for targeted interventions.
*   **Actionable recommendations:** The paper provides practical recommendations for mitigating harmful memorization and promoting beneficial memorization.
*   **Addresses a gap:** The paper fills a critical research gap by systematically studying memorization in the context of medical LLMs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited geographical scope:** The study is based on data from a single healthcare system (Yale New Haven). While large, it may not be representative of all healthcare systems globally. Future work could involve datasets from different regions and patient demographics to improve generalizability.
*   **Generalization of Categorization:** While the categorization is useful, the boundaries between categories might be blurry in practice and require nuanced human judgment.
*   **Limited discussion of mitigation techniques:** While recommendations are provided, the paper does not deeply investigate or test specific mitigation techniques in a hands-on fashion. Future research could explore and evaluate different de-identification, differential privacy, and adversarial training methods to mitigate harmful memorization.
*   **Metric Limitations:** The paper could benefit from a more granular evaluation of what *kinds* of patient information are being memorized, to allow for targeted future development on the most sensitive types of content.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a valuable and timely contribution to the field of medical AI. It highlights a critical issue (memorization) that could have serious implications for the responsible and ethical development of medical LLMs. The comprehensive analysis, the categorization framework, and the actionable recommendations are significant strengths. While there are limitations related to geographical scope and limited testing of mitigation techniques, the paper's contributions far outweigh its weaknesses.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper is a significant contribution to the field, directly addressing a critical gap in our understanding of LLMs in medicine. While some limitations exist in the scope and depth of exploration of specific mitigation techniques, the novelty and potential impact of the research justify a high score. The paper's findings are directly relevant to researchers, developers, and policymakers involved in the design and deployment of LLMs in healthcare. The comprehensive nature of the study, along with the use of real-world clinical data, further strengthens its contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### AdsQA: Towards Advertisement Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08621v1)
- **Authors**: Xinwei Long, Kai Tian, Peng Xu, Guoli Jia, Jingxuan Li, Sa Yang, Yihua Shao, Kaiyan Zhang, Che Jiang, Hao Xu, Yang Liu, Jiaheng Ma, Bowen Zhou
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have taken a great step towards AGI. Meanwhile, an increasing number of domain-specific problems such as math and programming boost these general-purpose models to continuously evolve via learning deeper expertise. Now is thus the time further to extend the diversity of specialized applications for knowledgeable LLMs, though collecting high quality data with unexpected and informative tasks is challenging. In this paper, we propose to use advertisement (ad) videos as a challenging test-bed to probe the ability of LLMs in perceiving beyond the objective physical content of common visual domain. Our motivation is to take full advantage of the clue-rich and information-dense ad videos' traits, e.g., marketing logic, persuasive strategies, and audience engagement. Our contribution is three-fold: (1) To our knowledge, this is the first attempt to use ad videos with well-designed tasks to evaluate LLMs. We contribute AdsQA, a challenging ad Video QA benchmark derived from 1,544 ad videos with 10,962 clips, totaling 22.7 hours, providing 5 challenging tasks. (2) We propose ReAd-R, a Deepseek-R1 styled RL model that reflects on questions, and generates answers via reward-driven optimization. (3) We benchmark 14 top-tier LLMs on AdsQA, and our \texttt{ReAd-R}~achieves the state-of-the-art outperforming strong competitors equipped with long-chain reasoning capabilities by a clear margin.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AdsQA, a new video question answering (QA) benchmark designed to evaluate the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) when applied to advertisement videos.  It argues that ad videos, with their implicit marketing logic and persuasive strategies, present a novel challenge beyond simple factual comprehension of visual content. The AdsQA dataset consists of 1,544 ad videos (over 10,000 clips, totaling 22.7 hours) and includes five tasks: Visual Concept Understanding, Emotion Recognition, Theme and Core Message Extraction, Persuasion Strategy Mining, and Potential Audience Modeling.  The paper also proposes ReAd-R, a Deepseek-R1 styled reinforcement learning (RL) model tailored for this domain, and benchmarks 14 LLMs on AdsQA, showing ReAd-R achieves state-of-the-art results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper is reasonably strong. The key contribution lies in identifying advertisement videos as a rich, but under-explored, domain for evaluating LLMs.  The AdsQA dataset addresses a gap in existing video QA benchmarks, which tend to focus on more straightforward factual content or task-oriented procedures. The tasks defined are tailored to advertising concepts and requires a level of implicit reasoning that goes beyond physical understanding.  The ReAd-R model is also a positive addition to the existing approaches. This approach is designed specifically for the nuances of ad video understanding.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is moderate.  While the immediate impact of understanding ads might seem narrow, the underlying skills being tested are highly relevant to broader AI research.  Understanding implicit intent, persuasive techniques, and target audiences are valuable for many real-world applications, including marketing analysis, content creation, and human-computer interaction. Showing that a specialized RL-based model outperforms generic LLMs highlights the potential for domain-specific fine-tuning and architecture design.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The AdsQA dataset is a valuable resource, providing a well-defined set of tasks and curated ad videos.
    *   The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing video QA benchmarks and motivates the need for a new dataset focused on implicit reasoning.
    *   The ReAd-R model demonstrates the effectiveness of RL for domain-specific fine-tuning of LLMs.
    *   The benchmarking provides a comprehensive evaluation of various LLMs on the AdsQA dataset, highlighting their strengths and weaknesses in this domain.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The connection between AdsQA and general AGI is overstated. While helpful for specific domains, these challenges may not apply to or support more generalized approaches.
    *   It would be more convincing to demonstrate the generalizability of ReAd-R to other domains outside of the advertisement video. This can further highlight the novelty and advantage of the research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by introducing a new and challenging video QA benchmark, AdsQA, and a tailored RL model, ReAd-R, that demonstrates state-of-the-art performance. While the significance of the research is somewhat limited to advertising video understanding, the underlying reasoning skills being evaluated are of broader interest.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LADB: Latent Aligned Diffusion Bridges for Semi-Supervised Domain Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08628v1)
- **Authors**: Xuqin Wang, Tao Wu, Yanfeng Zhang, Lu Liu, Dong Wang, Mingwei Sun, Yongliang Wang, Niclas Zeller, Daniel Cremers
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models excel at generating high-quality outputs but face challenges in data-scarce domains, where exhaustive retraining or costly paired data are often required. To address these limitations, we propose Latent Aligned Diffusion Bridges (LADB), a semi-supervised framework for sample-to-sample translation that effectively bridges domain gaps using partially paired data. By aligning source and target distributions within a shared latent space, LADB seamlessly integrates pretrained source-domain diffusion models with a target-domain Latent Aligned Diffusion Model (LADM), trained on partially paired latent representations. This approach enables deterministic domain mapping without the need for full supervision. Compared to unpaired methods, which often lack controllability, and fully paired approaches that require large, domain-specific datasets, LADB strikes a balance between fidelity and diversity by leveraging a mixture of paired and unpaired latent-target couplings. Our experimental results demonstrate superior performance in depth-to-image translation under partial supervision. Furthermore, we extend LADB to handle multi-source translation (from depth maps and segmentation masks) and multi-target translation in a class-conditioned style transfer task, showcasing its versatility in handling diverse and heterogeneous use cases. Ultimately, we present LADB as a scalable and versatile solution for real-world domain translation, particularly in scenarios where data annotation is costly or incomplete.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper:

The paper introduces Latent Aligned Diffusion Bridges (LADB), a semi-supervised framework for sample-to-sample translation using diffusion models. LADB addresses the challenge of data scarcity by aligning source and target distributions in a shared latent space. It leverages a pretrained source-domain diffusion model and trains a target-domain Latent Aligned Diffusion Model (LADM) on partially paired latent representations. This enables deterministic domain mapping with improved fidelity and diversity compared to unpaired methods, while requiring less paired data than fully supervised approaches. The paper demonstrates LADB's effectiveness in depth-to-image translation and further showcases its versatility through multi-source and multi-target translation applications.

Here's a critical evaluation of the paper's novelty and significance:

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Relevant Problem:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in diffusion model research: the data hunger of these models, especially in tasks like image-to-image translation where paired data is expensive or unavailable. The semi-supervised approach is a well-motivated solution to this problem.
*   **Novel Approach:** The core idea of aligning source and target domains in a latent space, combined with partially paired training of a diffusion model, represents a novel contribution. It leverages the power of pre-trained diffusion models on the source domain while adapting to the target domain with limited paired data. This is a reasonable improvement over fully unpaired or fully paired methods.
*   **Versatility:** The paper demonstrates the versatility of LADB by applying it to different tasks: depth-to-image translation, multi-source translation (depth and segmentation), and class-conditional style transfer. This demonstrates the general applicability of the framework.
*   **Deterministic Mapping:** The framework is deterministic, which is a desirable feature in many applications where a consistent mapping between domains is needed.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The paper includes experimental results that demonstrate the effectiveness of LADB, particularly in depth-to-image translation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty of Latent Space Alignment:** While the combination with diffusion models is novel, the concept of latent space alignment itself is not entirely new. Techniques like GANs have explored similar approaches for domain adaptation. The paper needs to convincingly demonstrate the advantages of aligning in a diffusion model latent space compared to existing alignment strategies.
*   **Complexity:** The framework seems relatively complex, involving a pretrained source diffusion model, a target LADM, and a latent space alignment component. The paper should provide a clear justification for this complexity and demonstrate that the performance gains are worth the added engineering effort.
*   **Comparisons:** The paper claims superior performance over other methods, but the experimental setup and the baselines used need to be carefully scrutinized. Are the baselines truly representative of the state-of-the-art? Does the paper provide a fair comparison, considering factors like model size, training resources, and hyperparameter tuning? A deeper dive into the comparison details is warranted. The lack of direct comparison to other semi-supervised image-to-image translation techniques is a significant omission.
*   **Scalability:** While the paper mentions scalability, it doesn't provide a detailed analysis of the computational costs associated with training and inference, especially for large-scale datasets and high-resolution images. The computational burden might be a limiting factor for some applications.
*   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** The reliance on a pretrained source-domain diffusion model might limit the applicability of LADB in scenarios where a suitable pretrained model is not available.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially useful framework for semi-supervised domain translation using diffusion models. The core idea of latent space alignment within a diffusion model framework is interesting. However, the novelty of the latent space alignment component itself is limited, and the paper needs to provide a more compelling justification for its complexity and demonstrate its advantages over existing domain adaptation techniques. The experimental validation, while promising, needs to be more thorough, with stronger baselines and a more detailed analysis of the computational costs. The paper has potential to influence the field of diffusion model applications, particularly in scenarios with limited data. However, the weaknesses mentioned above need to be addressed to fully realize its potential.

Score: 6.5

**Rationale for the Score:**

The score reflects a paper with moderate novelty and significance. The idea of LADB is promising, offering a practical solution for domain translation with limited paired data. However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon existing latent space alignment techniques. The paper demonstrates the potential of LADB through experiments, but the comparisons are not as strong as they could be, and the computational costs are not thoroughly analyzed. A score of 6.5 acknowledges the contribution of the paper while also highlighting its limitations and areas for improvement. A higher score would require stronger comparisons, a more thorough analysis of complexity and scalability, and a more compelling argument for the advantages of LADB over existing domain adaptation methods.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### BcQLM: Efficient Vision-Language Understanding with Distilled Q-Gated Cross-Modal Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08715v1)
- **Authors**: Sike Xiang, Shuang Chen, Amir Atapour-Abarghouei
- **Abstract**: As multimodal large language models (MLLMs) advance, their large-scale architectures pose challenges for deployment in resource-constrained environments. In the age of large models, where energy efficiency, computational scalability and environmental sustainability are paramount, the development of lightweight and high-performance models is critical for real-world applications. As such, we propose a lightweight MLLM framework for end-to-end visual question answering. Our proposed approach centres on BreezeCLIP, a compact yet powerful vision-language encoder optimised for efficient multimodal understanding. With only 1.2 billion parameters overall, our model significantly reduces computational cost while achieving performance comparable to standard-size MLLMs. Experiments conducted on multiple datasets further validate its effectiveness in balancing accuracy and efficiency. The modular and extensible design enables generalisation to broader multimodal tasks. The proposed lightweight vision-language framework is denoted as BcQLM (BreezeCLIP-enhanced Q-Gated Multimodal Language Model). It offers a promising path toward deployable MLLMs under practical hardware constraints. The source code is available at https://github.com/thico0224/BcQLM.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "BcQLM: Efficient Vision-Language Understanding with Distilled Q-Gated Cross-Modal Fusion." Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation and score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BcQLM, a lightweight multimodal large language model (MLLM) designed for efficient visual question answering (VQA) in resource-constrained environments. The core of BcQLM is BreezeCLIP, a compact vision-language encoder that replaces the standard BERT and ViT backbones in CLIP with inverted bottleneck transformer modules and is trained using a combination of contrastive learning and knowledge distillation from a larger CLIP model.  A Q-Gated Cross-Modal Fusion Module (Q-GCAM) is proposed to dynamically adjust the contribution of visual and textual features based on the question. Finally, a small LLaMA-3.2-1B model is used as a decoder. The paper presents experimental results on GQA, VQAv2, and VizWiz, demonstrating competitive performance compared to larger MLLMs, with a significantly reduced parameter count. The paper also includes ablation studies and efficiency analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Efficiency Focus:** The paper directly addresses a critical need in the MLLM field – the development of models that are practical for real-world deployment on resource-constrained devices. This is a very relevant and important direction.
*   **BreezeCLIP Innovation:** The BreezeCLIP architecture, with its distillation and lightweight components, represents a practical engineering approach to compressing vision-language models. Replacing BERT and ViT with more efficient modules is a logical step.
*   **Q-GCAM Contribution:** The Q-Gated Cross-Modal Fusion Module aims to improve the integration of visual and textual information in a question-aware manner, which is a plausible way to improve VQA accuracy and reasoning.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides a relatively thorough experimental evaluation across multiple VQA datasets, including comparisons against several existing MLLMs. The ablation studies also provide some insights into the contribution of the different components.
*   **Competitive Performance:** The model demonstrates competitive results with respect to more resource-intensive models in the VQA tasks.
*   **Reproducibility:** The provided code link encourages and enables further research based on this work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** While the paper presents a practical system, the individual components (knowledge distillation, bottleneck transformers, gated attention mechanisms) are not particularly novel. The primary contribution lies in the system integration and the overall engineering approach. It is an incremental advance rather than a groundbreaking contribution.
*   **LLaMA Decoder Freezing**: A notable limitation is the lack of finetuning for the LLaMA decoder. The paper acknowledges this and frames it as intentional, arguing that it makes the setup ideal to test the effectiveness of the fusion module, but it also potentially limits the performance that the overall system could achieve. By freezing the LLaMA model, they restrict the model from learning specific visual-language mappings relevant to the downstream VQA tasks.
*   **Limited Ablation Detail**: The ablation study presents limited information, not giving enough details to judge each module (eg. Q-GCAM) correctly.
*   **Incremental Gains:** It achieves competitive performance, but there is no extraordinary advantage over the other models.
*   **Dataset Bias:** VQA datasets are known to have biases, and relying solely on accuracy as an evaluation metric might not fully capture the model's ability to truly understand and reason about visual and textual information. Further analysis of the model's performance on different question types or specific visual concepts would be beneficial.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies primarily in its demonstration that it is possible to create a relatively efficient MLLM that performs reasonably well on VQA tasks. This is important for enabling MLLMs to be deployed in more practical settings. The paper could spur further research into lightweight MLLM architectures and training techniques.

**Overall:**

The paper provides a practical and useful contribution by presenting a lightweight MLLM that achieves competitive performance on VQA tasks. The novelty is somewhat limited, as the individual components are based on existing techniques. However, the paper addresses an important problem and provides a clear demonstration of a working system. The model's potential impact is not revolutionary, but it could serve as a solid foundation for future research in this area.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale for the Score:** The score reflects the paper's practical value and competitive results, tempered by its limited novelty and incremental improvements to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Data-driven generative simulation of SDEs using diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08731v1)
- **Authors**: Xuefeng Gao, Jiale Zha, Xun Yu Zhou
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces a new approach to generating sample paths of unknown stochastic differential equations (SDEs) using diffusion models, a class of generative AI models commonly employed in image and video applications. Unlike the traditional Monte Carlo methods for simulating SDEs, which require explicit specifications of the drift and diffusion coefficients, our method takes a model-free, data-driven approach. Given a finite set of sample paths from an SDE, we utilize conditional diffusion models to generate new, synthetic paths of the same SDE. To demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, we conduct a simulation experiment to compare our method with alternative benchmark ones including neural SDEs. Furthermore, in an empirical study we leverage these synthetically generated sample paths to enhance the performance of reinforcement learning algorithms for continuous-time mean-variance portfolio selection, hinting promising applications of diffusion models in financial analysis and decision-making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel data-driven approach for generating sample paths of unknown Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs) using diffusion models. Unlike traditional Monte Carlo methods that require explicit knowledge of drift and diffusion coefficients, the proposed method is model-free and relies solely on a finite set of sample paths from the unknown SDE. The method leverages conditional diffusion models to generate synthetic paths that statistically resemble the original SDE. The effectiveness of the approach is demonstrated through simulations comparing it to neural SDEs and a Monte Carlo score-based diffusion model. The paper also presents an empirical study where synthetically generated sample paths are used to enhance reinforcement learning algorithms for continuous-time mean-variance portfolio selection, suggesting promising applications in financial analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using conditional diffusion models to generate SDE paths from data, without explicitly learning the SDE's parameters, offers some novelty. While conditional diffusion models have been applied in other domains, their application to SDE path generation, particularly in the context of financial applications and reinforcement learning, is a valuable contribution. The use of conditional diffusion models to generate the stochastic *increments* of the SDE, taking advantage of the Markov property, seems appropriate. The comparison to [12] highlighting the use of a score neural network (as opposed to a Monte Carlo Estimator) is a key technical differentiation. However, the contribution would be greater with a deeper theoretical grounding as to why the neural score network offers specific advantages or is more suitable.

**Significance:** The paper has potential significance due to the broad applicability of SDEs in various fields. A method that can generate synthetic data from unknown SDEs has numerous applications, including:

*   **Simulation and testing:** Creating more diverse and realistic simulations for various systems modeled by SDEs.
*   **Data augmentation:** Enhancing the performance of machine learning models trained on limited data from SDEs.
*   **Financial modeling:** Generating realistic market scenarios for risk management and portfolio optimization, as demonstrated in the empirical study.
*   **Reinforcement Learning:** Expanding RL environments by including a wide variety of possible trajectories from synthetic SDE trajectories.

The empirical study on continuous-time mean-variance portfolio selection further strengthens the significance of the work by showcasing a practical application where the synthetic data generated by the proposed method can improve the performance of reinforcement learning algorithms. The claim that synthetic data allows the RL agent to explore more market scenarios and learn a robust policy seems likely.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper would benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of the proposed method. It lacks a rigorous justification for the choice of diffusion models and a formal proof of convergence or error bounds. Further analysis on the properties of the generated paths, such as ergodicity or stationarity, would be beneficial.
*   **Comparison to Existing Literature:** The discussion of existing literature in the model-free SDE simulation domain, particularly the comparison to Neural SDEs, could be more thorough. The paper should more clearly articulate the advantages and disadvantages of its approach compared to other model-free methods like GANs and normalizing flows beyond the advantages of diffusion models in general.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper does not discuss the sensitivity of the method to the choice of hyperparameters in the diffusion model.
*   **Scalability to high-dimensional SDEs:** While the empirical application is interesting, the paper doesn't thoroughly investigate the performance and limitations of the method in high-dimensional settings, beyond what is presented in example 2. Diffusion models can be computationally expensive, and the scalability of the proposed method to complex, high-dimensional SDEs remains unclear.
*   **Limited Empirical Evaluation:** While the experiments are well-designed, the range of SDEs considered are limited. More diverse examples would strengthen the claims made.
*   **Reliance on Markov Property:** The core idea of using autoregressive models is highly dependent on the underlying SDE exhibiting strong Markov properties.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach for generating SDE paths using diffusion models and demonstrates its potential for improving reinforcement learning in financial applications. This has some novelty and practical significance. However, the lack of a rigorous theoretical analysis, limited comparison to existing methods, and a relatively narrow empirical evaluation limit its impact. Considering these factors, a score of 6 is assigned.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Calibrating MLLM-as-a-judge via Multimodal Bayesian Prompt Ensembles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08777v1)
- **Authors**: Eric Slyman, Mehrab Tanjim, Kushal Kafle, Stefan Lee
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate text-to-image (TTI) generation systems, providing automated judgments based on visual and textual context. However, these "judge" models often suffer from biases, overconfidence, and inconsistent performance across diverse image domains. While prompt ensembling has shown promise for mitigating these issues in unimodal, text-only settings, our experiments reveal that standard ensembling methods fail to generalize effectively for TTI tasks. To address these limitations, we propose a new multimodal-aware method called Multimodal Mixture-of-Bayesian Prompt Ensembles (MMB). Our method uses a Bayesian prompt ensemble approach augmented by image clustering, allowing the judge to dynamically assign prompt weights based on the visual characteristics of each sample. We show that MMB improves accuracy in pairwise preference judgments and greatly enhances calibration, making it easier to gauge the judge's true uncertainty. In evaluations on two TTI benchmarks, HPSv2 and MJBench, MMB outperforms existing baselines in alignment with human annotations and calibration across varied image content. Our findings highlight the importance of multimodal-specific strategies for judge calibration and suggest a promising path forward for reliable large-scale TTI evaluation.
- **Summary**: ## Summary of the Paper

The paper addresses the problem of unreliable and biased evaluation of Text-to-Image (TTI) generation systems by Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Standard prompt ensembling, effective in text-only settings, doesn't generalize well to TTI evaluation. The authors propose Multimodal Mixture-of-Bayesian Prompt Ensembles (MMB), a novel approach that incorporates image clustering to dynamically weight prompts based on visual content. This method improves the accuracy and calibration of MLLM judges in pairwise preference judgments, as demonstrated on HPSv2 and MJBench datasets, leading to more reliable TTI evaluation.

## Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance

This paper presents a valuable and well-motivated contribution to the field of TTI evaluation. The problem of unreliable MLLM judges is a significant bottleneck in the development and deployment of high-quality TTI systems. The paper's novelty lies in the following:

*   **Addressing a specific weakness of MLLMs in TTI evaluation:** Existing prompt ensembling techniques, successful in unimodal text settings, are shown to be inadequate for the multimodal task of TTI evaluation. This insight is crucial.
*   **Image-aware prompt weighting:** The introduction of image clustering to inform prompt weighting is a genuinely novel approach. It allows the MLLM to tailor its judgment based on the visual characteristics of the generated images, addressing the issue of domain-specific biases.
*   **Bayesian Prompt Ensembles:** The use of a Bayesian approach provides a principled way to combine the judgments from different prompts and estimate the uncertainty of the judge. This is important for calibration.
*   **Empirical validation on relevant benchmarks:** The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of MMB on two established TTI benchmarks (HPSv2 and MJBench), providing strong empirical evidence for its claims.

However, some limitations and potential areas for improvement need to be considered:

*   **Complexity and computational cost:** The proposed MMB method introduces additional complexity due to the image clustering and Bayesian inference steps. The paper should more clearly address the computational overhead and potential scalability issues. While important, the computational overhead of judging is less significant than the training overhead of generation.
*   **Sensitivity to image clustering:** The performance of MMB depends heavily on the quality of the image clustering algorithm. The paper should provide more details about the clustering method used and analyze the impact of different clustering algorithms on the results.
*   **Generalizability to other TTI tasks:** The paper focuses on pairwise preference judgments. It is not clear how well MMB would generalize to other TTI evaluation tasks, such as image captioning or visual question answering.
*   **Choice of prompts:** The success of prompt ensembling relies on the diversity and quality of the chosen prompts. The paper should discuss the criteria used for selecting prompts and explore the impact of different prompt sets on the performance of MMB. Further ablation studies regarding the quality and variety of prompts would enhance the credibility of the paper.
*   **Limited comparisons:** While the paper compares MMB to existing baselines, including more recent and state-of-the-art methods for TTI evaluation could strengthen its claims. For instance, techniques that directly address the issue of bias in MLLM judges could provide a more challenging comparison.

Despite these limitations, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field by proposing a novel and effective approach for improving the reliability of MLLM judges in TTI evaluation. The idea of image-aware prompt weighting is particularly promising and could inspire further research in this area.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper addresses a well-defined and important problem in TTI evaluation. The proposed MMB method introduces a novel approach that significantly improves the accuracy and calibration of MLLM judges. The empirical results on HPSv2 and MJBench are convincing. However, the limitations related to computational cost, sensitivity to image clustering, generalizability, and limited comparisons prevent it from achieving a higher score. The novelty and practical benefits outweigh these limitations resulting in a score in the 7-8 range. A slightly higher score would be justified if a deeper analysis on the prompt selection was available.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Do All Autoregressive Transformers Remember Facts the Same Way? A Cross-Architecture Analysis of Recall Mechanisms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08778v1)
- **Authors**: Minyeong Choe, Haehyun Cho, Changho Seo, Hyunil Kim
- **Abstract**: Understanding how Transformer-based language models store and retrieve factual associations is critical for improving interpretability and enabling targeted model editing. Prior work, primarily on GPT-style models, has identified MLP modules in early layers as key contributors to factual recall. However, it remains unclear whether these findings generalize across different autoregressive architectures. To address this, we conduct a comprehensive evaluation of factual recall across several models -- including GPT, LLaMA, Qwen, and DeepSeek -- analyzing where and how factual information is encoded and accessed. Consequently, we find that Qwen-based models behave differently from previous patterns: attention modules in the earliest layers contribute more to factual recall than MLP modules. Our findings suggest that even within the autoregressive Transformer family, architectural variations can lead to fundamentally different mechanisms of factual recall.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper:

The paper investigates how different autoregressive Transformer models (GPT, LLaMA, Qwen, DeepSeek) store and retrieve factual information. It challenges the prevailing view that MLPs in early layers are the primary drivers of factual recall across all such models. The key finding is that, unlike GPT-style models, Qwen-based models rely more on attention modules in the initial layers for factual recall, indicating that architectural variations within the autoregressive Transformer family can lead to distinct mechanisms for encoding and accessing facts.

Now, a rigorous and critical evaluation of the paper's novelty and significance:

This paper directly addresses a gap in understanding how architectural differences within the autoregressive Transformer family affect factual knowledge storage and retrieval. The prior emphasis on GPT models, while informative, paints an incomplete picture. By systematically comparing several model families (including LLaMA, which is somewhat under-studied in this specific context, and the relatively newer Qwen and DeepSeek), the paper provides valuable comparative insights.

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in identifying a divergent behavior in Qwen-based models regarding the locus of factual recall. While previous work has focused on MLPs, the finding that attention modules in early layers are more crucial for Qwen is a novel contribution. This challenges the generalization of findings from GPT-centric studies. The analysis of DeepSeek is also a valuable addition, broadening the scope of the study. However, the method largely leverages existing techniques for factual recall analysis (e.g., ablation studies, prompting techniques). The core methodological contribution is relatively incremental, focusing on applying these existing techniques to a broader set of models and uncovering a notable difference.

**Significance:** The significance lies in several aspects:

1.  **Improved Understanding:** The paper deepens our understanding of how factual knowledge is represented in different types of Transformer architectures. This knowledge is crucial for developing more interpretable models and designing targeted editing techniques.
2.  **Practical Implications:** The findings suggest that techniques developed for editing factual knowledge in GPT models might not directly translate to other architectures, such as Qwen. This highlights the need for architecture-specific approaches to model editing and knowledge injection.
3.  **Research Direction:** The results motivate further research into the architectural factors that influence factual knowledge representation. It encourages the community to move beyond GPT-centric views and consider the diverse design choices within the Transformer family.

**Weaknesses:**

1.  **Depth of Explanation:** While the paper identifies the differing behavior of Qwen, it doesn't fully explain *why* this difference exists. A deeper investigation into the architectural differences between Qwen and other models and how these differences lead to different factual recall mechanisms would strengthen the paper. Simply observing the difference isn't enough; the paper needs to offer some plausible explanations or hypotheses. Are there specific attention mechanisms that are heavily relied upon? How does the pre-training data differ?
2.  **Scope of Models:** While the paper covers several models, it would be beneficial to include more diverse architectures, especially those with more radical departures from the standard Transformer (e.g., sparse attention, recurrent mechanisms).
3.  **Nature of Factual Recall:** The paper doesn't delve deeply into the specific *types* of facts recalled. Is the dependence on attention in Qwen specific to certain types of facts (e.g., relational facts vs. entity properties)? This nuance could further refine the understanding of the observed difference. The analysis assumes that factual recall is a monolithic phenomenon, which is likely an oversimplification.
4.  **Generalizability Concerns:** While the paper expands the scope beyond GPT, there are still limitations in terms of fully generalizing findings to all autoregressive models.

**Overall:** The paper makes a solid contribution by demonstrating that the mechanisms for factual recall are not universal across all autoregressive Transformer models. The identification of Qwen's distinct behavior is valuable. However, the paper could be significantly strengthened by providing more in-depth explanations for the observed differences, exploring the nuances of factual recall, and considering a wider range of architectures. The incremental nature of the methodology and the lack of in-depth explanation for the findings temper the overall impact.

Score: 6.5

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper earns a score of 6.5 because it identifies a significant and previously unobserved phenomenon (Qwen's reliance on attention for factual recall). This discovery challenges the existing understanding and points to the importance of considering architectural variations. However, the paper is limited by its lack of deeper explanation for the observed differences, its reliance on existing methodologies, and some limitations in the scope and analysis of factual recall. The findings are significant enough to warrant publication and further investigation, but the paper needs more depth to achieve a higher score. The novelty is moderate (identifying a difference using existing methods), and the impact is reasonably high due to the implications for model editing and interpretability, even if the "why" is not fully explained. A higher score would require stronger mechanistic explanations and a more nuanced analysis of the types of facts recalled.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Scaling Truth: The Confidence Paradox in AI Fact-Checking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08803v1)
- **Authors**: Ihsan A. Qazi, Zohaib Khan, Abdullah Ghani, Agha A. Raza, Zafar A. Qazi, Wassay Sajjad, Ayesha Ali, Asher Javaid, Muhammad Abdullah Sohail, Abdul H. Azeemi
- **Abstract**: The rise of misinformation underscores the need for scalable and reliable fact-checking solutions. Large language models (LLMs) hold promise in automating fact verification, yet their effectiveness across global contexts remains uncertain. We systematically evaluate nine established LLMs across multiple categories (open/closed-source, multiple sizes, diverse architectures, reasoning-based) using 5,000 claims previously assessed by 174 professional fact-checking organizations across 47 languages. Our methodology tests model generalizability on claims postdating training cutoffs and four prompting strategies mirroring both citizen and professional fact-checker interactions, with over 240,000 human annotations as ground truth. Findings reveal a concerning pattern resembling the Dunning-Kruger effect: smaller, accessible models show high confidence despite lower accuracy, while larger models demonstrate higher accuracy but lower confidence. This risks systemic bias in information verification, as resource-constrained organizations typically use smaller models. Performance gaps are most pronounced for non-English languages and claims originating from the Global South, threatening to widen existing information inequalities. These results establish a multilingual benchmark for future research and provide an evidence base for policy aimed at ensuring equitable access to trustworthy, AI-assisted fact-checking.
- **Summary**: ## Summary:

This paper investigates the performance of nine established large language models (LLMs) in fact-checking across 5,000 claims in 47 languages, previously assessed by professional fact-checkers. Using multiple prompting strategies and focusing on claims beyond the LLMs' training data, the study reveals a significant performance disparity. Smaller models exhibit high confidence despite lower accuracy, while larger models, though more accurate, display lower confidence. This creates a risk of biased information verification, especially for resource-limited organizations relying on smaller models. The study highlights performance gaps for non-English languages and claims from the Global South, potentially exacerbating existing information inequalities. It concludes by establishing a multilingual benchmark and advocating for policies promoting equitable access to trustworthy AI-assisted fact-checking.

## Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:

**Novelty:**

The paper presents several novel aspects. Firstly, its systematic evaluation of a diverse set of LLMs on a large-scale, multilingual fact-checking dataset is significant. While prior research has explored LLMs for fact verification, this study's breadth, encompassing both open and closed-source models across various sizes and architectures, is a notable contribution. Secondly, the focus on claims postdating the models' training cutoffs addresses a crucial limitation of existing evaluations. This tests the models' ability to generalize beyond their explicit knowledge. Thirdly, the inclusion of multiple prompting strategies mimicking both layman and professional fact-checker approaches adds a layer of realism to the evaluation. Finally, and perhaps most importantly, the examination of performance disparities across languages and geographical regions is crucial for understanding the potential for algorithmic bias in automated fact-checking.

**Significance:**

The findings have significant implications for the field of NLP, misinformation research, and policy-making. The observation of a "Dunning-Kruger effect" in smaller models is alarming, suggesting that readily accessible tools might be overconfident and inaccurate. The documented performance gaps for non-English languages and claims from the Global South underscore the risk of widening existing information inequalities through biased AI systems. The establishment of a multilingual benchmark is a valuable resource for future research, facilitating comparative evaluations of different fact-checking approaches. The paper's recommendations for policy interventions to ensure equitable access to trustworthy AI-assisted fact-checking are timely and important.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a diverse range of LLMs, prompting strategies, and languages, providing a more holistic understanding of LLM capabilities in fact-checking.
*   **Focus on Generalization:** Testing performance on claims postdating training data addresses a critical limitation of many LLM evaluations.
*   **Multilingual and Global South Focus:** The emphasis on non-English languages and claims from the Global South is crucial for addressing potential biases and ensuring equitable access to information.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a clear and detailed description of its methodology, allowing for replication and further research.
*   **Policy Implications:** The paper highlights the policy implications of its findings, advocating for interventions to promote equitable access to trustworthy AI-assisted fact-checking.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Domain Coverage:** While the dataset is multilingual, the paper doesn't explicitly address potential domain-specific variations in fact-checking performance. Different models might be better suited for verifying claims in specific areas like science, politics, or health. Future work should examine this.
*   **Reliance on Existing Fact-Checks:** The evaluation relies on previously fact-checked claims. While this provides a large-scale dataset, it might be subject to biases inherent in the original fact-checking process. For example, fact-checkers may be more likely to scrutinize certain types of claims or sources.
*   **Simplistic Prompting Strategies:** While the prompting strategies attempt to mimic citizen and professional fact-checker behavior, they might not fully capture the nuances of human reasoning and information seeking. More sophisticated prompting techniques could potentially improve LLM performance.
*   **Limited Explainability:** The paper focuses on overall accuracy and confidence scores but provides limited insights into the specific reasons for model failures. Analyzing error patterns could reveal valuable information about the models' limitations and areas for improvement.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some weaknesses, its strengths outweigh them significantly. The comprehensive evaluation, focus on generalization and multilingualism, and clear methodology make it a valuable contribution to the field. The findings regarding the Dunning-Kruger effect and the performance disparities across languages and regions are particularly alarming and highlight the urgent need for responsible development and deployment of AI-assisted fact-checking tools. The paper's discussion of policy implications is also timely and important. The provided multilingual benchmark is a valuable resource for future research. Taking these factors into account, I assign a score of **8**. This reflects the paper's significant novelty and potential impact, while acknowledging some limitations in domain coverage, potential biases in the underlying fact-checked data, and room for improvement in prompting strategies and explainability.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Evaluating LLMs Without Oracle Feedback: Agentic Annotation Evaluation Through Unsupervised Consistency Signals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08809v1)
- **Authors**: Cheng Chen, Haiyan Yin, Ivor Tsang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), when paired with prompt-based tasks, have significantly reduced data annotation costs and reliance on human annotators. However, evaluating the quality of their annotations remains challenging in dynamic, unsupervised environments where oracle feedback is scarce and conventional methods fail. To address this challenge, we propose a novel agentic annotation paradigm, where a student model collaborates with a noisy teacher (the LLM) to assess and refine annotation quality without relying on oracle feedback. The student model, acting as an unsupervised feedback mechanism, employs a user preference-based majority voting strategy to evaluate the consistency of the LLM outputs. To systematically measure the reliability of LLM-generated annotations, we introduce the Consistent and Inconsistent (CAI) Ratio, a novel unsupervised evaluation metric. The CAI Ratio not only quantifies the annotation quality of the noisy teacher under limited user preferences but also plays a critical role in model selection, enabling the identification of robust LLMs in dynamic, unsupervised environments. Applied to ten open-domain NLP datasets across four LLMs, the CAI Ratio demonstrates a strong positive correlation with LLM accuracy, establishing it as an essential tool for unsupervised evaluation and model selection in real-world settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of evaluating the quality of Large Language Model (LLM)-generated annotations in unsupervised environments, where oracle feedback is unavailable. It proposes a novel "agentic annotation evaluation" paradigm where a student model collaborates with a noisy teacher (the LLM) to assess annotation quality. The student model uses a preference-based majority voting strategy to evaluate the consistency of the LLM's outputs.  The paper introduces the "Consistent and Inconsistent (CAI) Ratio" as an unsupervised evaluation metric to quantify the reliability of LLM-generated annotations. This ratio effectively assesses annotation quality and helps in model selection without relying on labeled data. The authors validate their approach on ten open-domain NLP datasets using four LLMs, demonstrating a strong correlation between the CAI Ratio and LLM accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of using a student model to evaluate a teacher LLM's annotation quality in an unsupervised setting is fairly novel. The CAI Ratio is a new metric tailored for this scenario.  Existing unsupervised evaluation methods often rely on self-consistency checks *within* a single model, or agreement between multiple *independent* models. This approach uses a collaborative setup where the "student" provides feedback *to* the noisy "teacher" (the LLM), rather than just acting as a separate evaluator, potentially capturing more nuanced aspects of data structure. However, the majority voting strategy is fairly standard, but is adapted to user preferences here. The novelty comes from the agentic interaction and the specific ratio.

*   **Significance:** Accurate evaluation of LLM annotations is crucial for reducing reliance on expensive human annotation, especially in tasks like fine-tuning and real-time inference. An unsupervised metric like the CAI Ratio could have a substantial impact by enabling more efficient and cost-effective development cycles.  The demonstrated correlation with LLM accuracy suggests that the CAI Ratio is a useful tool for model selection in practical, real-world scenarios where labeled data is scarce. The claim of improving model evaluation and selection *without* oracle feedback is potentially valuable. However, the "preference-based majority voting" mechanism requires *some* user preferences to work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a timely and important problem.
    *   The approach is relatively simple to implement and understand.
    *   The experimental results show a promising correlation between the CAI Ratio and LLM accuracy.
    *   The paper explores using CAI for model selection.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dependence on user preferences, even a small amount (5% of the data), is a limitation. The method isn't truly *completely* unsupervised. It would be valuable to see how the CAI ratio changes as the amount of user preference data varies.
    *   The choice of MINILM as the student model might influence the results. A comparison with other embedding models would strengthen the findings. Also, is it truly *noisy*, or just disagreeing.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the cases where the CAI Ratio fails to identify the best-performing LLM. Understanding these failure cases would increase the robustness of the method.

*   **Potential Influence:** The CAI Ratio and the agentic annotation evaluation paradigm could influence how LLMs are evaluated and selected in unsupervised settings. The approach could be extended to other types of data annotation tasks and could inspire further research into unsupervised LLM evaluation techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially impactful approach to a significant problem. While the reliance on some user preferences is a limitation, the method offers a practical solution for unsupervised LLM evaluation and model selection. The demonstrated correlation with LLM accuracy and the clear explanation of the CAI ratio are strong points. The paper is well-written and the experimental results are convincing, but more experiments are needed on how well the evaluation does based on how 'noisy' the LLM truly is and how it is affected by lower levels of user preferences..

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Merge-of-Thought Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08814v2)
- **Authors**: Zhanming Shen, Zeyu Qin, Zenan Huang, Hao Chen, Jiaqi Hu, Yihong Zhuang, Guoshan Lu, Gang Chen, Junbo Zhao
- **Abstract**: Efficient reasoning distillation for long chain-of-thought (CoT) models is increasingly constrained by the assumption of a single oracle teacher, despite practical availability of multiple candidate teachers and growing CoT corpora. We revisit teacher selection and observe that different students have different "best teachers," and even for the same student the best teacher can vary across datasets. Therefore, to unify multiple teachers' reasoning abilities into student with overcoming conflicts among various teachers' supervision, we propose Merge-of-Thought Distillation (MoT), a lightweight framework that alternates between teacher-specific supervised fine-tuning branches and weight-space merging of the resulting student variants. On competition math benchmarks, using only about 200 high-quality CoT samples, applying MoT to a Qwen3-14B student surpasses strong models including DEEPSEEK-R1, QWEN3-30B-A3B, QWEN3-32B, and OPENAI-O1, demonstrating substantial gains. Besides, MoT consistently outperforms the best single-teacher distillation and the naive multi-teacher union, raises the performance ceiling while mitigating overfitting, and shows robustness to distribution-shifted and peer-level teachers. Moreover, MoT reduces catastrophic forgetting, improves general reasoning beyond mathematics and even cultivates a better teacher, indicating that consensus-filtered reasoning features transfer broadly. These results position MoT as a simple, scalable route to efficiently distilling long CoT capabilities from diverse teachers into compact students.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Merge-of-Thought Distillation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Merge-of-Thought Distillation (MoT), a novel distillation framework designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of language models.  Unlike traditional distillation methods that rely on a single "oracle" teacher, MoT leverages multiple teacher models with varying reasoning styles.  It works by iteratively fine-tuning student variants on teacher-specific data and then merging these variants in weight-space. This process aims to consolidate consistent reasoning signals while mitigating noise from individual teachers. Experiments on math benchmarks demonstrate that MoT can significantly enhance student performance, surpassing strong baselines and exhibiting robustness to distribution shifts and peer-level teachers. The paper also highlights that MoT mitigates catastrophic forgetting and promotes general reasoning abilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to knowledge distillation. While model merging is an existing technique, applying it iteratively within a distillation framework, specifically to combine the strengths of *multiple* reasoning teachers, is a significant contribution.  The observation that different students benefit from different teachers (and even on different datasets for the *same* student) is an important empirical finding that justifies the need for a multi-teacher approach like MoT.  This goes beyond simply ensembling outputs; it fundamentally changes the training process. The idea of alternating between teacher-specific training and weight-space merging to find a consensus reasoning landscape is innovative.

*   **Significance:** The paper has several elements of significance:
    *   **Performance Gains:** The reported performance improvements on challenging math benchmarks (AIME) are substantial.  Surpassing strong baselines like DeepSeek-R1, Qwen3-30B, and OpenAI-01 with a relatively small distilled dataset (200 examples) demonstrates the efficiency of MoT.
    *   **Robustness:** The demonstrated robustness to distribution shifts and peer-level teachers is valuable. This suggests that MoT can be applied in more realistic and diverse scenarios where teacher quality may vary.
    *   **Catastrophic Forgetting Mitigation:**  The observation that MoT reduces catastrophic forgetting and improves general reasoning skills is a crucial finding. This suggests that the framework promotes the learning of more transferable and robust reasoning features. The "student-as-teacher" experiment further strengthens this point.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the MoT framework.
    *   The experimental setup is thorough and includes several ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of different components. The per-round analysis in particular is insightful
    *   The analysis of training dynamics (comparing loss and AIME scores over steps) provides valuable insights into how MoT overcomes overfitting and achieves better regularization.
    *   The reverse trajectory merge probe provides convincing evidence about flattening the loss landscape.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While math benchmarks are valuable, the scope could be broadened to include other reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical reasoning) to assess the generalizability of MoT.
    *   The paper does not delve deeply into the theoretical underpinnings of why MoT works. While the empirical results are strong, a more formal analysis of the convergence properties of the iterative merge-and-train process would be beneficial. Specifically, it would be nice to have an understanding of conditions under which a consensus emerges.
    *   The selection of teachers may have affected the outcome, even with the experiments included in the appendix. It would be useful to see more analysis on how different quality and variance in teacher models affect results of MoT.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The MoT framework has the potential to become a standard technique for distilling reasoning capabilities in language models, especially in situations where multiple teacher models are available.
    *   The finding that consensus reasoning signals are more robust and transferable could influence the design of future distillation methods.
    *   The observation regarding teacher selection could influence future benchmark design and experimental evaluation.

**Score: 8.5**

**Justification:**

The paper introduces a novel and effective distillation framework (MoT) that addresses a practical challenge in real-world scenarios: leveraging multiple teacher models with diverse reasoning styles. The empirical results on challenging math benchmarks are compelling, demonstrating significant performance gains and robustness. The mitigation of catastrophic forgetting and the improvement in general reasoning skills further enhance the paper's significance. While the paper could benefit from a more detailed theoretical analysis and broader evaluation across different reasoning tasks, the novelty, significance, and thoroughness of the experiments justify a high score. The practical implications of MoT and its potential influence on future distillation techniques are considerable. The approach significantly advances the state of the art, providing a clear and usable framework that solves several limitations of current distillation methods.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Building High-Quality Datasets for Portuguese LLMs: From Common Crawl Snapshots to Industrial-Grade Corpora
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08824v1)
- **Authors**: Thales Sales Almeida, Rodrigo Nogueira, Helio Pedrini
- **Abstract**: The performance of large language models (LLMs) is deeply influenced by the quality and composition of their training data. While much of the existing work has centered on English, there remains a gap in understanding how to construct effective training corpora for other languages. We explore scalable methods for building web-based corpora for LLMs. We apply them to build a new 120B token corpus in Portuguese that achieves competitive results to an industrial-grade corpus. Using a continual pretraining setup, we study how different data selection and preprocessing strategies affect LLM performance when transitioning a model originally trained in English to another language. Our findings demonstrate the value of language-specific filtering pipelines, including classifiers for education, science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM), as well as toxic content. We show that adapting a model to the target language leads to performance improvements, reinforcing the importance of high-quality, language-specific data. While our case study focuses on Portuguese, our methods are applicable to other languages, offering insights for multilingual LLM development.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper addresses the challenge of building high-quality training corpora for large language models (LLMs) in languages other than English. It presents a scalable method for constructing web-based corpora and applies it to create a 120B token Portuguese corpus, demonstrating competitive performance against industrial-grade data. The authors investigate the impact of various data selection and preprocessing techniques, particularly language-specific filters for STEM content and toxicity, using a continual pretraining approach. Their results highlight the importance of these techniques for adapting English-trained LLMs to new languages and underscore the need for tailored data strategies in multilingual LLM development. While the focus is on Portuguese, the methods are presented as broadly applicable to other languages.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the increasingly important area of multilingual LLM development. Its strength lies in its pragmatic approach, focusing on scalable methods for building training corpora, a crucial and often under-emphasized aspect of LLM research. The evaluation through continual pretraining on a sizable 120B token corpus provides empirical evidence supporting the efficacy of their proposed data selection and filtering techniques. The inclusion of language-specific toxicity and STEM filters demonstrates a practical understanding of the nuances required when dealing with languages other than English, which is often overlooked in more generic approaches. The competitive performance against an industrial-grade corpus further strengthens the paper's claims.

However, the paper has some weaknesses. While the methods are presented as broadly applicable, the empirical evaluation is limited to Portuguese. A more compelling argument for generalizability would require demonstrating effectiveness on at least one more typologically different language (e.g., a non-Indo-European language with different grammatical structures). Furthermore, the paper lacks detail regarding the exact configuration of the "industrial-grade" corpus used as a baseline. Without that information, the claim of "competitive results" is somewhat weakened. The descriptions of the STEM and toxicity classifiers, which form a crucial part of the data filtering pipeline, could be more detailed. Providing information on the training data, evaluation metrics, and architectures of these classifiers would significantly improve the paper's reproducibility and impact. While the paper shows the benefits of language-specific filters, it doesn't deeply analyze *why* these filters are effective beyond the general statement of improving data quality. A deeper investigation into the characteristics of the web data being filtered and the specific errors that the filters mitigate would strengthen the analysis. Finally, while the paper highlights the value of continual pretraining, it does not rigorously compare this approach against alternative fine-tuning strategies or training from scratch.

The novelty, while not revolutionary, is significant. The paper presents a practical and scalable methodology for building high-quality, language-specific corpora for LLMs, focusing on Portuguese. The emphasis on language-specific data selection and preprocessing techniques, particularly the inclusion of STEM and toxicity filters tailored to the target language, adds value to existing corpus construction approaches. This research addresses a clear and pressing need in the field of multilingual LLM development. It offers valuable insights and a practical framework for researchers and practitioners aiming to build or adapt LLMs for languages beyond English.

In summary, the paper presents a practical and valuable contribution to the field of multilingual LLM development. While there are areas for improvement, the research provides empirical evidence for the effectiveness of language-specific data filtering and demonstrates a scalable methodology for building high-quality training corpora. The focus on Portuguese addresses a gap in the literature and offers a useful case study for adapting LLMs to other languages.
