    The work contributes to the growing body of literature on using LLMs for Table QA, providing a practical and effective pipeline for tackling real-world datasets. The inclusion of iterative refinement and verification steps is particularly valuable for improving the reliability of LLM-generated SQL queries. The performance gains shown over baseline methods are significant, indicating the potential of this approach.

**Rationale for Score:**

While the paper presents a well-engineered and effective NL-to-SQL pipeline, its novelty is primarily in the integration and tuning of existing techniques, rather than introducing groundbreaking new methods.  The dependency on powerful, potentially closed-source LLMs, also limits its overall significance and reproducibility. The limited error analysis and benchmarking against other state-of-the-art systems reduces its potential impact and influence. However, the practical performance improvements on a challenging benchmark and the comprehensive methodology justify a positive, but not exceptionally high, score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Jupiter: Enhancing LLM Data Analysis Capabilities via Notebook and Inference-Time Value-Guided Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09245v1)
- **Authors**: Shuocheng Li, Yihao Liu, Silin Du, Wenxuan Zeng, Zhe Xu, Mengyu Zhou, Yeye He, Haoyu Dong, Shi Han, Dongmei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown great promise in automating data science workflows, but existing models still struggle with multi-step reasoning and tool use, which limits their effectiveness on complex data analysis tasks. To address this, we propose a scalable pipeline that extracts high-quality, tool-based data analysis tasks and their executable multi-step solutions from real-world Jupyter notebooks and associated data files. Using this pipeline, we introduce NbQA, a large-scale dataset of standardized task-solution pairs that reflect authentic tool-use patterns in practical data science scenarios. To further enhance multi-step reasoning, we present Jupiter, a framework that formulates data analysis as a search problem and applies Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to generate diverse solution trajectories for value model learning. During inference, Jupiter combines the value model and node visit counts to efficiently collect executable multi-step plans with minimal search steps. Experimental results show that Qwen2.5-7B and 14B-Instruct models on NbQA solve 77.82% and 86.38% of tasks on InfiAgent-DABench, respectively-matching or surpassing GPT-4o and advanced agent frameworks. Further evaluations demonstrate improved generalization and stronger tool-use reasoning across diverse multi-step reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "JUPITER: Enhancing LLM Data Analysis Capabilities via Notebook and Inference-Time Value-Guided Search":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) in complex, multi-step data analysis tasks. It introduces two key contributions:

1.  **NbQA Dataset:** A large-scale dataset of standardized task-solution pairs extracted from real-world Jupyter notebooks. The NbQA dataset aims to capture authentic tool-use patterns in data science.
2.  **JUPITER Framework:**  A framework that formulates data analysis as a search problem and employs Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to generate diverse solution trajectories. A value model, trained on the interaction trajectories, guides the search process, aiming to efficiently collect executable multi-step plans during inference.

The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that LLMs fine-tuned on NbQA and using the JUPITER framework achieve significant improvements in data analysis capabilities, matching or surpassing the performance of GPT-4 and advanced agent frameworks on benchmarks like InfiAgent-DABench, DSBench, and AIME. The results also highlight improved generalization and stronger tool-use reasoning across various multi-step reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a well-defined approach for improving LLM performance in data analysis, combining a new dataset (NbQA) with a search-based framework (JUPITER). While MCTS and value models are not novel in themselves, their application to the data analysis domain, particularly in the context of Jupyter notebooks and LLMs, is reasonably novel. The automated pipeline for generating the NbQA dataset from Jupyter notebooks is a solid engineering contribution. The combination of these two elements, along with the demonstration of improved performance, suggests a genuine advance.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a practically significant problem: automating data analysis workflows. The development of NbQA and JUPITER addresses a key bottleneck in LLM application to data science, namely the difficulty in handling complex, multi-step reasoning tasks. The experimental results, especially surpassing GPT-4 in some tasks, showcase the potential impact of the proposed approach. The emphasis on using open-source models, making the approach more accessible, adds to its significance. Furthermore, the generalization results on different datasets (DSBench and AIME) strengthen the applicability of the work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Dataset:** The NbQA dataset is a valuable contribution to the field, providing a resource for training and evaluating LLMs in data analysis. The automated extraction pipeline is a key enabler.
    *   **Framework:** The JUPITER framework offers a principled approach to complex data analysis by formulating it as a search problem, leveraging MCTS and value models for efficient exploration.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The paper provides comprehensive experimental evidence, showcasing the effectiveness of the proposed approach on multiple datasets and comparing it with state-of-the-art baselines.
    *   **Emphasis on Open-Source:** The work contributes to democratizing data analysis automation by improving open-source LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The JUPITER framework, while effective, introduces significant complexity with its use of MCTS and value models. This may create barriers to adoption for practitioners. The computational overhead is likely substantial.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** While large, the NbQA dataset is limited to the domain of Jupyter notebooks.  It's unclear how well the models trained on NbQA would generalize to data analysis tasks performed in other environments (e.g., scripting environments).
    *   **Search Space Dependency:**  The performance of JUPITER is highly dependent on the ability of the base LLM to generate reasonable thought-action pairs. If the base LLM produces poor candidates, the MCTS will be ineffective. The exploration/exploitation trade-off also may be difficult to tune.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM-based data analysis. The NbQA dataset could become a widely used benchmark. The JUPITER framework provides a foundation for developing more sophisticated data analysis agents.  The paper's success in improving open-source LLMs will likely encourage further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the points above, a score of **8** is appropriate.

*   The paper is well-written, technically sound, and addresses a practically important problem. The proposed NbQA dataset and JUPITER framework demonstrate significant improvements in LLM-based data analysis capabilities. The experimental results are compelling, and the focus on open-source models increases the impact of the work.
*   However, the complexity of the JUPITER framework, limitations of the NbQA dataset, and dependency on LLM-generated candidates detract somewhat from its overall score. While the paper presents some generalization results, additional studies should be done to determine how well the approach can be applied in diverse scenarios. Furthermore, the paper could offer some insight into the computational overhead introduced.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### DATE: Dynamic Absolute Time Enhancement for Long Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09263v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Yuan, Yang Yang, Yehui Yang, Zach Cheng
- **Abstract**: Long video understanding remains a fundamental challenge for multimodal large language models (MLLMs), particularly in tasks requiring precise temporal reasoning and event localization. Existing approaches typically adopt uniform frame sampling and rely on implicit position encodings to model temporal order. However, these methods struggle with long-range dependencies, leading to critical information loss and degraded temporal comprehension. In this paper, we propose Dynamic Absolute Time Enhancement (DATE) that enhances temporal awareness in MLLMs through the Timestamp Injection Mechanism (TIM) and a semantically guided Temporal-Aware Similarity Sampling (TASS) strategy. Specifically, we interleave video frame embeddings with textual timestamp tokens to construct a continuous temporal reference system. We further reformulate the video sampling problem as a vision-language retrieval task and introduce a two-stage algorithm to ensure both semantic relevance and temporal coverage: enriching each query into a descriptive caption to better align with the vision feature, and sampling key event with a similarity-driven temporally regularized greedy strategy. Our method achieves remarkable improvements w.r.t. absolute time understanding and key event localization, resulting in state-of-the-art performance among 7B and 72B models on hour-long video benchmarks. Particularly, our 7B model even exceeds many 72B models on some benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DATE (Dynamic Absolute Time Enhancement), a method designed to improve the temporal reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for long video understanding. DATE addresses the limitations of uniform frame sampling and implicit position encodings, which often lead to information loss and poor temporal comprehension in long videos. The approach includes two main components: (1) a Timestamp Injection Mechanism (TIM) that explicitly inserts textual timestamp tokens within the video frame embeddings to create a continuous temporal reference system, and (2) a Temporal-Aware Similarity Sampling (TASS) strategy, which reframes video sampling as a vision-language retrieval task. TASS uses a two-stage algorithm: descriptive caption generation to improve vision feature alignment, and a similarity-driven, temporally regularized greedy strategy to sample key events while maintaining temporal coverage.  The experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on several long video benchmarks, especially in absolute time understanding and key event localization, even surpassing larger 72B models with its 7B model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach demonstrates reasonable novelty. Explicitly injecting timestamps via the TIM is a relatively straightforward idea, but the specific implementation and its integration with the MLLM architecture is well-executed. The TASS strategy is more innovative; reframing frame selection as a vision-language retrieval task and the use of descriptive captions enhances semantic relevance. The temporal regularization component is crucial for ensuring temporal diversity, a common challenge in sampling strategies. The novelty primarily lies in the specific combination and orchestration of these components rather than radical invention of any single component.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very significant and practical challenge in the field of multimodal learning: effectively processing and understanding long videos. The demonstrated improvements in absolute time understanding and key event localization are impactful, as these are fundamental aspects of video comprehension. The fact that a 7B model can outperform 72B models on some benchmarks is compelling and has practical implications for resource-constrained deployments.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The experimental section is thorough, with evaluations on multiple challenging long video benchmarks. The ablation studies clearly demonstrate the contribution of each component (TIM and TASS).  The comparison against strong baselines, including large-scale models, bolsters the claim of state-of-the-art performance.
    *   **Clear and well-structured approach:** The DATE method is described in a clear and concise manner, making it relatively easy to understand and implement. The algorithm descriptions and visualizations are helpful.
    *   **Addressing limitations of existing methods:** The paper clearly articulates the shortcomings of existing frame sampling strategies and positional encoding techniques for long videos, providing strong motivation for the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **TIM's potential limitations**: As a relatively simple approach, it's difficult to say how TIM impacts models of different architectures or when time isn't necessarily a crucial feature.
    *   **Computational cost:** The TASS component relies on generating image captions from the original LLM model, potentially increasing computational overhead. While the paper mentions efficiency measures, a more detailed analysis of inference time, especially for very long videos, is needed. Also, how much is it dependent on the strength of LLM being used to generate a caption?
    *   **Generalizability:** While DATE demonstrates good results on several benchmarks, the long-term generalizability of the approach should be further studied when the data is different.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of long video understanding by providing a practical and effective approach to improve temporal reasoning in MLLMs. The ideas presented could inspire further research into more sophisticated frame sampling strategies and explicit temporal modeling techniques. The results showing the effectiveness of a smaller model are important and could shift focus towards more efficient architectures.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**
The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by introducing DATE, a practical and well-validated method for enhancing temporal understanding in MLLMs for long videos. While the individual components are not radically new, their combination and effectiveness in addressing a significant problem earn the paper a good score. The strong empirical results and clear presentation strengthen its overall impact. However, the relatively incremental nature of the innovation, and a lack of a more nuanced computational analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score.

*   The paper is technically sound and builds upon existing work in a logical and convincing manner.
*   The evaluation is comprehensive and demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed method.
*   The novelty is reasonable and the significance is clear.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Harnessing Uncertainty: Entropy-Modulated Policy Gradients for Long-Horizon LLM Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09265v1)
- **Authors**: Jiawei Wang, Jiacai Liu, Yuqian Fu, Yingru Li, Xintao Wang, Yuan Lin, Yu Yue, Lin Zhang, Yang Wang, Ke Wang
- **Abstract**: In long-horizon tasks, recent agents based on Large Language Models (LLMs) face a significant challenge that sparse, outcome-based rewards make it difficult to assign credit to intermediate steps. Previous methods mainly focus on creating dense reward signals to guide learning, either through traditional reinforcement learning techniques like inverse reinforcement learning or by using Process Reward Models for step-by-step feedback. In this paper, we identify a fundamental problem in the learning dynamics of LLMs: the magnitude of policy gradients is inherently coupled with the entropy, which leads to inefficient small updates for confident correct actions and potentially destabilizes large updates for uncertain ones. To resolve this, we propose Entropy-Modulated Policy Gradients (EMPG), a framework that re-calibrates the learning signal based on step-wise uncertainty and the final task outcome. EMPG amplifies updates for confident correct actions, penalizes confident errors, and attenuates updates from uncertain steps to stabilize exploration. We further introduce a bonus term for future clarity that encourages agents to find more predictable solution paths. Through comprehensive experiments on three challenging agent tasks, WebShop, ALFWorld, and Deep Search, we demonstrate that EMPG achieves substantial performance gains and significantly outperforms strong policy gradient baselines. Project page is at https://empgseed-seed.github.io/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Entropy-Modulated Policy Gradients (EMPG), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to address the credit assignment problem in long-horizon Large Language Model (LLM) agent training. EMPG tackles the issue of sparse rewards by leveraging the intrinsic uncertainty of the agent's "reasoning-action" steps.  The framework includes two key components: (1) Self-Calibrating Gradient Scaling, which dynamically adjusts the policy gradient based on step-wise uncertainty, amplifying updates for confident correct actions and attenuating updates for uncertain ones; and (2) a Future Clarity Bonus, which encourages actions that lead to more predictable future states. Through experiments on WebShop, ALFWorld, and Deep Search, the authors demonstrate that EMPG significantly improves performance over strong baselines like GRPO and DAPO. The paper provides a theoretical justification for EMPG and highlights its ability to enhance both learning efficiency and stability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of using entropy as a modulator for policy gradients is not entirely novel.  Previous works have explored entropy minimization or maximization for various purposes. However, the specific combination of *self-calibrating* gradient scaling with a *future clarity bonus*, motivated by a rigorous analysis of the link between entropy and gradient magnitude, *and* targeted explicitly for the long-horizon credit assignment problem in LLM agents, constitutes a significant advance.  The paper goes beyond simply using entropy as a reward signal and provides a sophisticated mechanism for recalibrating the learning signal. The theoretical justification, while not groundbreaking in its mathematical complexity, does provide a solid foundation for the method.

*   **Significance:** The credit assignment problem is a major bottleneck in training effective LLM agents for complex tasks. The empirical results clearly demonstrate the practical benefits of EMPG in improving performance across several challenging benchmarks. The ablations highlight the importance of both the gradient scaling and future clarity bonus components. The analysis of learning dynamics and the comparison of step-level vs. token-level entropy also add valuable insights. The observation that EMPG avoids the performance plateaus commonly seen with baseline methods suggests it enables the agent to escape local optima and discover better policies. In terms of application the work opens avenues for improving LLM agents in interactive web navigation, search, and other environments with sparse, outcome-based feedback.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-motivated approach.
    *   Rigorous theoretical analysis connecting entropy and gradient magnitude.
    *   Comprehensive empirical evaluation on multiple challenging tasks and model sizes.
    *   Ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of individual components.
    *   Insightful analysis of learning dynamics and step-level entropy.
    *   Well-written and presented paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The mathematical derivations are somewhat standard and do not represent a significant theoretical breakthrough.
    *   The impact of hyperparameters `k` and `k'` isn't fully explored and might require extensive tuning for new tasks. While the paper notes how normalization reduces hyperparameter tuning, the potential for task-specific optimisation remains.
    *   While the experiments use various model sizes the method should also be evaluated on significantly larger models to evaluate scaling.
    *   The reliance on `process_token_sequences` from the `VeRL` framework, although convenient, may make it harder to reproduce the method. Providing a minimal standalone implementation in supplementary materials could improve reproducibility.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by providing a practical and theoretically grounded solution to the credit assignment problem in LLM agent training. The EMPG framework can be readily adopted and adapted by other researchers and practitioners working on similar problems. The paper's insights into the relationship between entropy and gradient magnitude may also inspire further research on uncertainty-aware learning methods.

**Overall:**

Despite some limitations, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field. The EMPG framework is well-motivated, theoretically sound, and empirically effective. The paper's findings are likely to be of interest to a broad audience of researchers and practitioners working on LLM agents and reinforcement learning.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fusing Knowledge and Language: A Comparative Study of Knowledge Graph-Based Question Answering with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09272v1)
- **Authors**: Vaibhav Chaudhary, Neha Soni, Narotam Singh, Amita Kapoor
- **Abstract**: Knowledge graphs, a powerful tool for structuring information through relational triplets, have recently become the new front-runner in enhancing question-answering systems. While traditional Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) approaches are proficient in fact-based and local context-based extraction from concise texts, they encounter limitations when addressing the thematic and holistic understanding of complex, extensive texts, requiring a deeper analysis of both text and context. This paper presents a comprehensive technical comparative study of three different methodologies for constructing knowledge graph triplets and integrating them with Large Language Models (LLMs) for question answering: spaCy, Stanford CoreNLP-OpenIE, and GraphRAG, all leveraging open source technologies. We evaluate the effectiveness, feasibility, and adaptability of these methods by analyzing their capabilities, state of development, and their impact on the performance of LLM-based question answering. Experimental results indicate that while OpenIE provides the most comprehensive coverage of triplets, GraphRAG demonstrates superior reasoning abilities among the three. We conclude with a discussion on the strengths and limitations of each method and provide insights into future directions for improving knowledge graph-based question answering.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper compares three open-source methodologies (spaCy, Stanford CoreNLP-OpenIE, and GraphRAG) for extracting knowledge graph triplets from text and integrating them with Large Language Models (LLMs) for question answering. The study evaluates their effectiveness, feasibility, and adaptability, focusing on their impact on LLM performance. The findings suggest that OpenIE extracts the most comprehensive set of triplets, while GraphRAG exhibits better reasoning capabilities. The paper concludes by discussing the strengths and weaknesses of each method and outlining future research directions for knowledge graph-based question answering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This paper addresses a relevant and important problem in the field of question answering and knowledge representation: how to effectively leverage knowledge graphs, especially when dealing with complex and large documents where traditional RAG methods falter. The study's focus on open-source tools is commendable, as it promotes reproducibility and accessibility for researchers.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of traditional RAG and positions knowledge graphs as a potential solution for enhancing question answering with complex texts.
*   **Comparative Analysis:** The direct comparison of three different methodologies (spaCy, OpenIE, and GraphRAG) offers valuable insights into their respective strengths and weaknesses.
*   **Practical Focus:** The paper evaluates the "feasibility" and "adaptability" of each method, which are crucial considerations for real-world applications.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The use of experimental results to support the claims adds credibility to the analysis.
*   **Future Directions:** The paper concludes with a discussion of potential improvements and future research directions, which can guide further investigations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Comparison:** While the three methods are distinct, there are many other knowledge graph construction techniques (both open-source and commercial) that could have been included for a more comprehensive analysis. The chosen methods might not be representative of the entire landscape.
*   **Lack of Depth in Evaluation Metrics:** The paper mentions "effectiveness," "feasibility," and "adaptability," but the specific metrics used to evaluate these aspects are not described in sufficient detail. What constitutes 'superior reasoning abilities'? How were they measured?
*   **LLM Choice and Tuning:** The paper doesn't provide sufficient information on the LLM used and how it was fine-tuned or prompted. This could significantly impact the results. The LLM choice is a crucial element in any RAG comparison.
*   **Missing Baseline:** While the paper compares different KG extraction methods, it lacks a clear baseline like standard RAG without a KG. This makes it difficult to ascertain the true value-add of KG integration.
*   **Limited Novelty in Methodology:** The individual components (spaCy, OpenIE, GraphRAG) are all established techniques. The novelty lies primarily in *combining* them with LLMs for question answering. Therefore, the contribution is more incremental than revolutionary.
*   **Generalizability:** The paper doesn't sufficiently address the generalizability of the findings. The conclusions might be specific to the chosen dataset and the specific question answering tasks considered.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a useful comparative analysis of different knowledge graph construction methodologies for enhancing question answering. However, the limited scope of comparison, lack of detailed evaluation metrics, and absence of a baseline weaken the conclusions and overall impact. While the study provides valuable insights, it doesn't represent a significant breakthrough in the field. The novelty is incremental, and the generalizability of the findings is uncertain.

Score: 6

**Rationale:**

The paper is worthwhile because it explores an important area and offers practical insights into using different KG construction methods with LLMs. However, the limitations discussed above prevent it from achieving a higher score. A score of 6 reflects the paper's contributions while acknowledging its shortcomings in terms of scope, methodological rigor, and overall novelty. The lack of a solid baseline and the limited depth of analysis in the evaluation metrics are the most significant drawbacks. A more comprehensive comparison, including a strong baseline and more detailed metrics, would have significantly increased the paper's impact and justified a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Tree-OPO: Off-policy Monte Carlo Tree-Guided Advantage Optimization for Multistep Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09284v1)
- **Authors**: Bingning Huang, Tu Nguyen, Matthieu Zimmer
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in reasoning with large language models (LLMs) have shown the effectiveness of Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) for generating high-quality intermediate trajectories, particularly in math and symbolic domains. Inspired by this, we explore how MCTS-derived trajectories, traditionally used for training value or reward models, can be repurposed to improve policy optimization in preference-based reinforcement learning (RL). Specifically, we focus on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a recent algorithm that enables preference-consistent policy learning without value networks. We propose a staged GRPO training paradigm where completions are derived from partially revealed MCTS rollouts, introducing a novel tree-structured setting for advantage estimation. This leads to a rich class of prefix-conditioned reward signals, which we analyze theoretically and empirically. Our initial results indicate that while structured advantage estimation can stabilize updates and better reflect compositional reasoning quality, challenges such as advantage saturation and reward signal collapse remain. We propose heuristic and statistical solutions to mitigate these issues and discuss open challenges for learning under staged or tree-like reward structures.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper explores using Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) trajectories to improve policy optimization in preference-based reinforcement learning (RL), specifically within the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) framework. Instead of using MCTS trajectories solely for reward/value model training, the authors propose using them directly to generate a structured, tree-like reward signal for advantage estimation within GRPO. They introduce a staged GRPO training paradigm using partial MCTS rollouts and analyze the resulting prefix-conditioned reward signals. The initial results suggest that this structured advantage estimation can stabilize policy updates, but challenges like advantage saturation and reward signal collapse need to be addressed with heuristics and statistical methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty lies in the specific combination of using MCTS *trajectories directly* for advantage estimation in a *preference-based RL setting* using GRPO, rather than just for value/reward model learning. Leveraging partial MCTS rollouts to create a tree-structured reward signal is also a relatively novel approach. The concept of prefix-conditioned rewards within this context adds another layer of originality. However, the individual components (MCTS, GRPO, advantage estimation, preference-based RL) are well-established. The innovation lies in the creative integration of these elements.

**Significance:**

The potential significance stems from the possibility of leveraging the compositional reasoning capabilities of MCTS to improve policy learning in preference-based RL, especially in tasks requiring reasoning. If successful, it could lead to more efficient and effective preference learning, bypassing the often unreliable step of explicit reward modeling. The identification of challenges like advantage saturation and reward signal collapse is also a valuable contribution, as it highlights potential pitfalls in this approach.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies a challenge in preference-based RL and proposes a specific solution.
*   **Novel Integration:** The combination of MCTS, GRPO, and structured advantage estimation is a relatively novel approach.
*   **Theoretical and Empirical Analysis:** The paper includes both theoretical analysis of the reward signals and empirical evaluation of the proposed method.
*   **Identification of Challenges:** The authors openly acknowledge and analyze the challenges faced, such as advantage saturation and reward signal collapse.
*   **Well-Defined Staged Training Paradigm:** The paper provides a clear description of the training process.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Initial Results:** While the results indicate potential for stabilization, the paper acknowledges that challenges remain. The improvements aren't overwhelmingly convincing based on the summary provided.
*   **Heuristic and Statistical Solutions:** The reliance on heuristics and statistical solutions to mitigate the identified challenges suggests that the underlying theoretical understanding of the problem might be incomplete.
*   **Lack of Baseline Comparisons:** The summary doesn't mention comparisons against standard GRPO or other preference-based RL baselines, making it difficult to gauge the true effectiveness of the proposed method. Ideally, it would demonstrate clear improvements over standard GRPO to really showcase how using MCTS-derived rewards improves training.
*   **Dependency on MCTS Quality:** The performance is likely heavily dependent on the quality of the MCTS rollouts, which might be a bottleneck in more complex domains. The paper needs to address the computational cost and scalability implications.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel integration of existing techniques and identifies important challenges in a specific context, the preliminary results and reliance on heuristics prevent a higher score. The lack of convincing empirical improvements over standard GRPO hinders the significance of the work. The paper's impact relies heavily on future research that builds upon these findings and addresses the limitations.

Score: 6

Rigorous rationale: The paper's novelty is above average due to the specific combination of techniques. However, the impact is currently limited due to the preliminary nature of the results and the open challenges. The score of 6 reflects the potential of the idea and the clear identification of important issues, but also the need for further research to demonstrate its practical effectiveness and advantages over existing methods. A score above 7 would require a more substantial demonstration of improved performance and a more thorough analysis of the scalability and robustness of the approach.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Visual Programmability: A Guide for Code-as-Thought in Chart Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09286v1)
- **Authors**: Bohao Tang, Yan Ma, Fei Zhang, Jiadi Su, Ethan Chern, Zhulin Hu, Zhixin Wang, Pengfei Liu, Ya Zhang
- **Abstract**: Chart understanding presents a critical test to the reasoning capabilities of Vision-Language Models (VLMs). Prior approaches face critical limitations: some rely on external tools, making them brittle and constrained by a predefined toolkit, while others fine-tune specialist models that often adopt a single reasoning strategy, such as text-based chain-of-thought (CoT). The intermediate steps of text-based reasoning are difficult to verify, which complicates the use of reinforcement-learning signals that reward factual accuracy. To address this, we propose a Code-as-Thought (CaT) approach to represent the visual information of a chart in a verifiable, symbolic format. Our key insight is that this strategy must be adaptive: a fixed, code-only implementation consistently fails on complex charts where symbolic representation is unsuitable. This finding leads us to introduce Visual Programmability: a learnable property that determines if a chart-question pair is better solved with code or direct visual analysis. We implement this concept in an adaptive framework where a VLM learns to choose between the CaT pathway and a direct visual reasoning pathway. The selection policy of the model is trained with reinforcement learning using a novel dual-reward system. This system combines a data-accuracy reward to ground the model in facts and prevent numerical hallucination, with a decision reward that teaches the model when to use each strategy, preventing it from defaulting to a single reasoning mode. Experiments demonstrate strong and robust performance across diverse chart-understanding benchmarks. Our work shows that VLMs can be taught not only to reason but also how to reason, dynamically selecting the optimal reasoning pathway for each task.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper:

This paper introduces a novel "Code-as-Thought" (CaT) approach for chart understanding in Vision-Language Models (VLMs). It addresses the limitations of existing methods that either rely on brittle external tools or use inflexible, single-strategy reasoning (like text-based CoT). The core innovation is "Visual Programmability," where the VLM learns to adaptively choose between CaT (representing the chart symbolically in code) and direct visual reasoning, based on the complexity of the chart and question. The selection policy is trained using reinforcement learning with a dual-reward system: data accuracy and decision accuracy. Experiments show improved performance across diverse chart understanding benchmarks, demonstrating the model's ability to select the optimal reasoning pathway.

Here's a rigorous and critical evaluation of the paper's novelty and significance:

The paper presents a well-motivated and executed approach to chart understanding. Its novelty lies primarily in the introduction of "Visual Programmability" and the adaptive framework that allows the VLM to choose between Code-as-Thought (CaT) and direct visual reasoning. This is a significant departure from previous methods that tend to rely on a single, pre-defined reasoning strategy or brittle external tools.

**Strengths:**

*   **Adaptive Reasoning:** The core idea of adaptively selecting between CaT and direct visual analysis is a strong one. It addresses the inherent limitations of purely symbolic or purely visual approaches. Simple charts can be handled visually, while complex ones benefit from the structured representation offered by code.
*   **Dual-Reward System:** The use of a dual-reward system (data accuracy and decision accuracy) in reinforcement learning is crucial. The data accuracy reward prevents the model from hallucinating numerical values and ensures factual correctness, which is a common problem in VLMs. The decision reward is equally important as it guides the model to learn when to effectively use each reasoning pathway. This addresses a key challenge in training such adaptive systems.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper demonstrates strong empirical results across diverse chart understanding benchmarks. This suggests that the proposed approach is not only theoretically sound but also practically effective.
*   **Addressing Limitations of Existing Approaches:** The paper explicitly addresses the limitations of existing methods, such as the brittleness of tool-based approaches and the inflexibility of single-strategy models, making a clear case for its contribution.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity of Code Generation:** While CaT offers verifiability and precision, the complexity of generating accurate and efficient code for all types of charts remains a significant challenge. The paper doesn't fully delve into the intricacies and potential limitations of code generation. The prompt engineering required for effective code generation is implicitly addressed but not explicitly discussed.
*   **Scalability and Generalization:** While the benchmarks used are diverse, it's unclear how well the approach scales to even more complex and novel chart types that may not be well-represented in the training data. The generalizability of the learned visual programmability is a potential concern.
*   **Reliance on Reinforcement Learning:** Training with reinforcement learning can be notoriously difficult, requiring careful tuning and potentially leading to instability. The paper doesn't fully discuss the challenges encountered during the RL training process or potential alternative training strategies. While the use of dual rewards is a strength, it also adds to the complexity of training.
*   **Evaluation of Visual Programmability:** While the paper claims the VLM learns Visual Programmability, it doesn't offer a comprehensive evaluation of the *learned property* itself. Are there interpretable features that drive the decision-making process? Further analysis of the learned selection policy would strengthen the paper.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes significantly to the field of Vision-Language Reasoning, particularly in the context of chart understanding. The idea of adaptive reasoning pathways, guided by Visual Programmability, offers a promising direction for addressing the limitations of existing approaches. The dual-reward RL training strategy is also a valuable contribution that can be applied to other adaptive reasoning tasks. The work could potentially influence the design of future VLMs that are more flexible, robust, and capable of selecting the optimal reasoning strategy for a given task.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a valuable contribution, the challenges related to code generation complexity, scalability, and the intricacies of RL training, as well as the limited analysis of the learned Visual Programmability, temper the impact. It's a step forward, but further research is needed to address these limitations fully. The innovation in combining CaT with direct visual analysis and learning to choose between them, however, justifies a strong score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### What You Code Is What We Prove: Translating BLE App Logic into Formal Models with LLMs for Vulnerability Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09291v1)
- **Authors**: Biwei Yan, Yue Zhang, Minghui Xu, Runyu Pan, Jinku Li, Xiuzhen Cheng
- **Abstract**: The application layer of Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE) is a growing source of security vulnerabilities, as developers often neglect to implement critical protections such as encryption, authentication, and freshness. While formal verification offers a principled way to check these properties, the manual effort of constructing formal models makes it impractical for large-scale analysis. This paper introduces a key insight: BLE application security analysis can be reframed as a semantic translation problem, i.e., from real-world code to formal models. We leverage large language models (LLMs) not to directly detect vulnerabilities, but to serve as translators that convert BLE-specific code into process models verifiable by tools like ProVerif. We implement this idea in VerifiaBLE, a system that combines static analysis, prompt-guided LLM translation, and symbolic verification to check three core security features: encryption, randomness, and authentication. Applied to 1,050 Android BLE apps, VerifiaBLE uncovers systemic weaknesses: only 10.2\% of apps implement all three protections, while 53.9\% omit them entirely. Our work demonstrates that using LLMs as structured translators can lower the barrier to formal methods, unlocking scalable verification across security-critical domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "What You 'Code' Is What We 'Prove': Translating BLE App Logic into Formal Models with LLMs for Vulnerability Detection" introduces VerifiaBLE, a system that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) as semantic translators to convert Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE) application code into formal models that can be verified using tools like ProVerif.  The goal is to bridge the gap between real-world, often unstructured BLE code and the rigorous but demanding world of formal verification, thus enabling large-scale security analysis. The system combines static analysis to extract relevant code paths, LLM-powered translation to generate formal models, and symbolic verification to identify vulnerabilities related to encryption, randomness (nonces), and authentication.  The authors evaluated VerifiaBLE on 1,050 Android BLE apps, demonstrating the prevalence of security weaknesses at the application layer and validating the effectiveness of their approach through manual analysis and real-device attacks.  The results highlight that many BLE apps lack fundamental security protections, exposing users to risks like eavesdropping, replay attacks, and man-in-the-middle attacks. The study also reveals correlations between security practices, app popularity, and developer consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs as *structured translators* rather than vulnerability *detectors* is novel and represents a key strength of the paper. Instead of asking the LLM to directly find vulnerabilities (a task at which they are unreliable), the authors intelligently leverage LLMs' code understanding and generation capabilities to create formal models. The integration of static analysis, LLMs, and formal verification in a pipeline specifically tailored for BLE application security is also a significant contribution. The use of Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) to reduce errors is a worthwhile refinement.
* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical problem: the difficulty of applying formal verification to real-world, heterogeneous application code.  BLE application layer security is an increasingly important area, given the proliferation of IoT devices and the tendency for developers to overlook security best practices. By demonstrating that LLMs can lower the barrier to formal methods, the authors unlock the potential for scalable verification in security-critical domains.  The large-scale empirical study (1,050 apps) provides valuable insights into the state of BLE application security and highlights the widespread neglect of fundamental protections.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of BLE application security and the limitations of existing approaches.
    * **Sound technical approach:** The design of VerifiaBLE is well-reasoned and leverages the strengths of each component (static analysis, LLM translation, formal verification).
    * **Thorough evaluation:** The experiments are extensive and include manual validation, comparisons to existing tools, and real-device attack demonstrations.
    * **Valuable findings:** The empirical study reveals significant security weaknesses in real-world BLE apps and provides insights into factors influencing security adoption.
    * **Generalizability:** The authors correctly point out that the core idea of using LLMs as semantic translators could be applied to other domains.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited scope of security properties:** The analysis focuses on encryption, randomness, and authentication.  While these are important, other security aspects (e.g., authorization, access control, input validation) are not considered.  The justification for this focus could be stronger.  Is it purely feasibility-driven, or are there specific reasons these three are most critical?
    * **Obfuscated code exclusion:** The exclusion of obfuscated code limits the applicability of the tool, as obfuscation is a common practice.  The paper acknowledges this limitation but does not explore potential mitigation strategies.
    * **Reliance on GPT-4:** The performance of VerifiaBLE is likely heavily dependent on the capabilities of GPT-4.  The paper should discuss the potential impact of using different or less powerful LLMs. The sensitivity of results to prompt engineering isn't fully addressed.
    * **False positives:** The authors identify the source of FPs stemming from incomplete/ineffective implementation of security features which *superficially* appear correct. This exposes an underlying difficulty in the automated translation: effectively capturing nuances and subtle errors in code.
* **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of IoT security, as it provides a practical and scalable approach to verifying the security of BLE applications. It also demonstrates the potential of LLMs to bridge the gap between software engineering and formal verification, opening up new avenues for research and development.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field.  While there are limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The careful design, thorough evaluation, and valuable findings make it a strong paper. The approach is practical and scalable, and the key insight of using LLMs as semantic translators has broader implications.  The authors present a convincing case for the effectiveness of their approach and provide a solid foundation for future research.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### LightAgent: Production-level Open-source Agentic AI Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09292v1)
- **Authors**: Weige Cai, Tong Zhu, Jinyi Niu, Ruiqi Hu, Lingyao Li, Tenglong Wang, Xiaowu Dai, Weining Shen, Liwen Zhang
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs), Multi-agent Systems (MAS) have achieved significant progress in various application scenarios. However, substantial challenges remain in designing versatile, robust, and efficient platforms for agent deployment. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{LightAgent}, a lightweight yet powerful agentic framework, effectively resolving the trade-off between flexibility and simplicity found in existing frameworks. LightAgent integrates core functionalities such as Memory (mem0), Tools, and Tree of Thought (ToT), while maintaining an extremely lightweight structure. As a fully open-source solution, it seamlessly integrates with mainstream chat platforms, enabling developers to easily build self-learning agents. We have released LightAgent at \href{https://github.com/wxai-space/LightAgent}{https://github.com/wxai-space/LightAgent}
- **Summary**: The paper introduces LightAgent, a lightweight and open-source multi-agent system framework. It aims to address the trade-off between flexibility and simplicity in existing frameworks by integrating key functionalities like Memory, Tools, and Tree of Thought (ToT) while maintaining a streamlined structure. The authors claim LightAgent seamlessly integrates with mainstream chat platforms, enabling developers to easily build self-learning agents. The repository is publicly available.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a common problem in the field of multi-agent systems built with LLMs: the complexity of existing frameworks. The attempt to create a lightweight solution is inherently valuable, as it can lower the barrier to entry for researchers and developers and potentially improve efficiency.

However, the paper lacks substantial detail to assess the true novelty and significance of LightAgent. Several critical aspects are not clearly articulated:

*   **Depth of Functionality:** While mentioning Memory, Tools, and ToT, the paper doesn't specify how these are implemented. Are these novel implementations or wrappers around existing solutions (e.g., Langchain components, existing vector databases)? Without this information, it's difficult to judge the technical contribution. Simply listing features doesn't equate to novelty.
*   **Comparison to Existing Frameworks:** The paper claims to address a trade-off between flexibility and simplicity. However, it doesn't explicitly compare LightAgent to other existing frameworks like Langchain, AutoGen, or crewAI. A detailed comparative analysis highlighting the advantages and disadvantages of LightAgent relative to these alternatives is crucial for establishing its value proposition. The claims of "effectively resolving the trade-off" and "seamlessly integrates" need to be substantiated with concrete examples and benchmarks.
*   **Evaluation and Performance:** The paper lacks any empirical evaluation. Without quantitative results on metrics like task completion rate, inference latency, memory usage, or development time for specific tasks, it's impossible to determine whether LightAgent actually achieves its goals of efficiency and ease of use. Claims of "self-learning agents" also require demonstration and qualification. What learning mechanisms are employed? How effective are they?
*   **Target Audience and Use Cases:** While the paper mentions chat platforms, it lacks a clear specification of the intended use cases. Is it designed for research, education, or production deployments? This information is important for evaluating the design choices and the potential impact of LightAgent.
*   **Open Source Value:** While being open-source is a positive aspect, the value of the repository depends on the quality of the code, documentation, and community support. A simple link to the repository is insufficient; evidence of active development and a well-maintained project are needed.

**In summary:** The paper identifies a valid problem and proposes a potentially useful solution. However, the lack of technical detail, comparative analysis, empirical evaluation, and clear articulation of use cases significantly weakens its contribution. The claims made are not sufficiently supported by evidence. While the open-source nature is a plus, the impact will depend on the actual quality and adoption of the codebase. Without concrete evidence demonstrating a significant advantage over existing solutions, the novelty and significance are limited.

Score: 4

**Rationale:** The paper identifies a relevant issue and presents a potential solution, which earns it some credit. However, the lack of depth, rigorous evaluation, and comparative analysis prevents a higher score. The project's open-source nature and potential for future development offer some promise, but the current presentation lacks the necessary substance to be considered a significant contribution to the field. A score of 4 reflects a promising start with substantial room for improvement and further validation.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 4/10

### From scratch to silver: Creating trustworthy training data for patent-SDG classification using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09303v1)
- **Authors**: Grazia Sveva Ascione, Nicolò Tamagnone
- **Abstract**: Classifying patents by their relevance to the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) is crucial for tracking how innovation addresses global challenges. However, the absence of a large, labeled dataset limits the use of supervised learning. Existing methods, such as keyword searches, transfer learning, and citation-based heuristics, lack scalability and generalizability. This paper frames patent-to-SDG classification as a weak supervision problem, using citations from patents to SDG-tagged scientific publications (NPL citations) as a noisy initial signal. To address its sparsity and noise, we develop a composite labeling function (LF) that uses large language models (LLMs) to extract structured concepts, namely functions, solutions, and applications, from patents and SDG papers based on a patent ontology. Cross-domain similarity scores are computed and combined using a rank-based retrieval approach. The LF is calibrated via a custom positive-only loss that aligns with known NPL-SDG links without penalizing discovery of new SDG associations. The result is a silver-standard, soft multi-label dataset mapping patents to SDGs, enabling the training of effective multi-label regression models. We validate our approach through two complementary strategies: (1) internal validation against held-out NPL-based labels, where our method outperforms several baselines including transformer-based models, and zero-shot LLM; and (2) external validation using network modularity in patent citation, co-inventor, and co-applicant graphs, where our labels reveal greater thematic, cognitive, and organizational coherence than traditional technological classifications. These results show that weak supervision and semantic alignment can enhance SDG classification at scale.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper:

This paper presents a novel weak supervision framework for classifying patents according to their relevance to the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). It addresses the lack of labeled data by using citations to SDG-tagged scientific publications (NPL citations) as a noisy initial signal. To improve on this signal, the authors develop a composite labeling function (LF) powered by large language models (LLMs). This LF extracts structured concepts (functions, solutions, applications) from both patents and SDG papers based on a patent ontology and calculates cross-domain similarity scores using a rank-based retrieval approach. A custom positive-only loss function calibrates the LF. The resulting silver-standard, soft multi-label dataset allows for training effective multi-label regression models. The approach is validated both internally (against held-out NPL labels) and externally (using network modularity). The results demonstrate improved performance and thematic coherence compared to baselines, including transformer models, zero-shot LLMs, and traditional technological classifications.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and potentially significant contribution to the field of patent classification and SDG alignment. Its strength lies in addressing a key challenge: the lack of labeled data for training supervised models. The proposed weak supervision framework, utilizing NPL citations and enhanced by LLM-driven concept extraction, offers a clever workaround.

Here's a breakdown of the strengths:

*   **Addressing a Real Problem:** The paper tackles a crucial issue: how to classify patents according to SDGs in the absence of large labeled datasets. This is important for tracking innovation towards sustainable development.
*   **Innovative Approach:** The combination of NPL citations (a known but limited signal) with LLM-based concept extraction is a novel and promising approach. The use of a patent ontology and the extraction of specific structured concepts (functions, solutions, applications) are particularly insightful.
*   **Technical Soundness:** The paper outlines a clear methodology, including the construction of the labeling function, the calibration using a positive-only loss, and the validation strategies.
*   **Comprehensive Validation:** The use of both internal and external validation provides a strong case for the effectiveness of the proposed method. The network modularity analysis is a particularly compelling way to demonstrate the improved thematic coherence of the generated labels.
*   **Benchmarking:** Comparison against transformer models and zero-shot LLMs provides context and demonstrates the advantage of the proposed approach.

However, the paper also has some limitations:

*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The performance of the method heavily relies on the accuracy and effectiveness of the LLMs in extracting relevant concepts. While the paper describes the methodology, a more detailed discussion of the specific LLMs used, prompting strategies, and error analysis would be beneficial. How robust is the approach to different LLMs and variations in prompt design?
*   **Domain Specificity:** While the use of a patent ontology is a strength, it also introduces a potential limitation. How well does the ontology generalize to different technological domains and new or emerging technologies? Does the ontology need to be constantly updated?
*   **Scalability Concerns:** While the paper claims scalability, processing a large number of patents and SDG papers with LLMs can be computationally expensive. A more thorough discussion of the computational resources required and potential optimization strategies would be helpful.
*   **"Silver Standard" Limitations:** The resulting dataset is still a "silver standard," meaning it's based on noisy labels. The paper acknowledges this, but it's important to remember that the accuracy and reliability of any downstream models trained on this dataset will be limited by the quality of the labels. How does the silver standard impact the performance of downstream tasks like patent search or technology forecasting?
*   **Lack of Transparency:** The paper mentions the use of "proprietary LLMs" which creates a reproducibility issue. Details concerning the selection of LLMs and training process should be disclosed.

**Overall:**

The paper offers a significant advance in automating patent-to-SDG classification. The LLM-enhanced weak supervision approach is a creative solution to the data scarcity problem. However, the reliance on specific (and potentially proprietary) LLMs, the domain specificity of the ontology, and the limitations of the "silver standard" labels need to be considered. While significant, a more detailed exploration of these limitations would elevate the paper's contribution.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Can Multimodal LLMs See Materials Clearly? A Multimodal Benchmark on Materials Characterization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09307v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengzhao Lai, Youbin Zheng, Zhenyang Cai, Haonan Lyu, Jinpu Yang, Hongqing Liang, Yan Hu, Benyou Wang
- **Abstract**: Materials characterization is fundamental to acquiring materials information, revealing the processing-microstructure-property relationships that guide material design and optimization. While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have recently shown promise in generative and predictive tasks within materials science, their capacity to understand real-world characterization imaging data remains underexplored. To bridge this gap, we present MatCha, the first benchmark for materials characterization image understanding, comprising 1,500 questions that demand expert-level domain expertise. MatCha encompasses four key stages of materials research comprising 21 distinct tasks, each designed to reflect authentic challenges faced by materials scientists. Our evaluation of state-of-the-art MLLMs on MatCha reveals a significant performance gap compared to human experts. These models exhibit degradation when addressing questions requiring higher-level expertise and sophisticated visual perception. Simple few-shot and chain-of-thought prompting struggle to alleviate these limitations. These findings highlight that existing MLLMs still exhibit limited adaptability to real-world materials characterization scenarios. We hope MatCha will facilitate future research in areas such as new material discovery and autonomous scientific agents. MatCha is available at https://github.com/FreedomIntelligence/MatCha.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces MatCha, a new benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the ability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to understand materials characterization images. The dataset contains 1,500 questions spanning four key stages of materials research and covering 21 distinct tasks requiring expert-level knowledge. The authors evaluated several state-of-the-art MLLMs on MatCha and found a significant performance gap compared to human experts, particularly on questions requiring higher-level reasoning and visual perception. The paper concludes that current MLLMs have limited adaptability to real-world materials characterization scenarios and hopes MatCha will stimulate future research in areas like material discovery and autonomous scientific agents.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation and introduction of the MatCha benchmark. While MLLMs have been applied to materials science, the creation of a dedicated dataset specifically focused on materials characterization image understanding fills a crucial gap. Prior works often relied on generic image datasets or narrowly focused, task-specific datasets. MatCha's comprehensiveness, spanning multiple stages of materials research and including diverse characterization techniques, makes it a significant contribution. The emphasis on expert-level questions and the inclusion of complex visual reasoning tasks further strengthens its novelty. However, the types of questions, although domain-specific, likely draw upon existing question-answering and visual reasoning paradigms used in the wider computer vision community.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from its potential to accelerate the development of MLLMs for materials science. The current state-of-the-art models show a clear deficiency in understanding materials characterization data, indicating a substantial need for improvement. MatCha provides a valuable tool for researchers to evaluate and compare different MLLM architectures and training strategies. The benchmark can also drive the development of specialized training techniques, such as domain adaptation or transfer learning, to improve the performance of MLLMs on materials science tasks.

The creation of a robust benchmark directly tackles a limitation in the field, allowing for measurable progress and focusing research efforts. The paper's conclusion highlights exciting avenues for future research, including new material discovery and the creation of autonomous scientific agents. However, the direct practical impact of a benchmark dataset is inherently indirect. The true significance will be determined by how the dataset is used and how effectively it guides the development of new and improved MLLMs for materials science.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies a gap in the application of MLLMs to materials characterization image understanding.
*   **Comprehensive dataset:** MatCha covers a wide range of materials science tasks and characterization techniques.
*   **Expert-level questions:** The questions require domain-specific knowledge and sophisticated visual reasoning.
*   **Clear evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation of state-of-the-art MLLMs on MatCha.
*   **Open access:** The dataset is publicly available, promoting reproducibility and further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited model improvement techniques explored:** The paper primarily focuses on evaluating existing models and only briefly explores simple prompting strategies. More sophisticated fine-tuning or pre-training approaches could be explored.
*   **Dataset bias:** While the paper aims for comprehensive coverage, potential biases within the dataset (e.g., imaging techniques, materials classes) need to be carefully considered and addressed in future work. Details regarding this are not well-defined.
*   **Real-world impact assessment:** The paper discusses potential applications but lacks concrete examples of how MatCha can directly contribute to real-world materials science research or industrial applications, only hinting at the long-term goal.
*   **Lack of statistical significance tests comparing results.** A deeper statistical analyses of the results (specifically when demonstrating degradation in model performance) could aid to bolster the claims being made.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has limitations, the creation of the MatCha benchmark addresses a critical gap in the application of MLLMs to materials science. It offers a structured and standardized way to assess the performance of these models on tasks that require expert-level knowledge and sophisticated visual reasoning. The benchmark's comprehensive coverage and public availability contribute significantly to the field's progress. However, the lack of in-depth exploration of model improvement techniques and potential dataset biases somewhat limits its impact. While the dataset will likely catalyze new developments, the specific contributions cannot be thoroughly stated at this point. The lack of statistical rigor when conducting analyses and limited results also hinders the value of the paper.

Therefore, a score of **7** is appropriate. It represents a significant contribution with the potential to drive progress in the field, but it also acknowledges the need for further research and refinement. The real value will be seen in the extent to which the community adopts and utilizes MatCha to create substantially better MLLMs in the future.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Adaptive ML Benchmarks: Web-Agent-Driven Construction, Domain Expansion, and Metric Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09321v1)
- **Authors**: Hangyi Jia, Yuxi Qian, Hanwen Tong, Xinhui Wu, Lin Chen, Feng Wei
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled the emergence of general-purpose agents for automating end-to-end machine learning (ML) workflows, including data analysis, feature engineering, model training, and competition solving. However, existing benchmarks remain limited in task coverage, domain diversity, difficulty modeling, and evaluation rigor, failing to capture the full capabilities of such agents in realistic settings. We present TAM Bench, a diverse, realistic, and structured benchmark for evaluating LLM-based agents on end-to-end ML tasks. TAM Bench features three key innovations: (1) A browser automation and LLM-based task acquisition system that automatically collects and structures ML challenges from platforms such as Kaggle, AIcrowd, and Biendata, spanning multiple task types and data modalities (e.g., tabular, text, image, graph, audio); (2) A leaderboard-driven difficulty modeling mechanism that estimates task complexity using participant counts and score dispersion, enabling scalable and objective task calibration; (3) A multi-dimensional evaluation framework incorporating performance, format compliance, constraint adherence, and task generalization. Based on 150 curated AutoML tasks, we construct three benchmark subsets of different sizes -- Lite, Medium, and Full -- designed for varying evaluation scenarios. The Lite version, with 18 tasks and balanced coverage across modalities and difficulty levels, serves as a practical testbed for daily benchmarking and comparative studies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TAM-Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the capabilities of LLM-based agents in automating end-to-end machine learning (ML) tasks. TAM-Bench addresses limitations in existing benchmarks by focusing on: (1) automated task collection and standardization from diverse platforms (Kaggle, Alcrowd, Biendata); (2) leaderboard-based difficulty modeling using participant statistics and score dispersion; and (3) a multi-dimensional evaluation framework assessing performance, format compliance, constraint adherence, and generalization ability. The benchmark comprises 150 curated AutoML tasks, with subsets of varying sizes (Lite, Medium, Full) tailored for different evaluation scenarios. A key feature is the "Lite" version, designed for practical daily benchmarking.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper exhibits moderate novelty. While existing benchmarks exist for ML automation (MLEBench, MLAgentBench), TAM-Bench's contributions lie in its automated task acquisition, leaderboard-driven difficulty modeling, and comprehensive evaluation framework. The automated task collection addresses a key bottleneck in benchmark creation. The Leaderboard-driven difficulty modeling, based on statistical properties of existing competition results, provides a scalable approach to creating a well-graded difficulty distribution across benchmark datasets. The holistic evaluation considering multiple metrics (performance, format, constraint adherence) is a welcome improvement.

*Significance:*

The potential significance is substantial, especially if TAM-Bench becomes a widely adopted standard. By providing a diverse, realistic, and structured benchmark, it can drive research and development in LLM-powered ML agents. Automated task collection promotes scalability. The leaderboard-based difficulty modeling allows objective calibration and fair comparison of agents' capabilities across diverse complexities. The multi-dimensional evaluation ensures that improvements translate to real-world applicability rather than simply reward hacking.

*Strengths:*

*   **Automated task acquisition:** Reduces manual effort and promotes scalability.
*   **Leaderboard-based difficulty modeling:** Provides an objective and scalable way to estimate task complexity.
*   **Multi-dimensional evaluation:** Captures a more complete picture of agent capabilities.
*   **Practicality:** The Lite version offers a manageable testbed for daily evaluations.
*   **Diversity**: Wider coverage of various data modalities and task types than existing benchmarks.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Reliance on competition data:** The benchmark's quality depends on the quality and representativeness of the selected ML competitions. There might be biases in the types of tasks represented.
*   **Potential for data contamination**: While the paper mentions excluding pre-2023 tasks, the risk of data contamination from pretraining corpora is hard to completely eliminate.
*   **Complexity in Implementation**: Constructing and maintaining the automated benchmarking pipeline requires significant engineering effort.
*   **Evaluation Metrics Can be More Sophisticated**. While multi-dimensional, the metrics used are still relatively basic.

*Justification for Score:*

TAM-Bench is a significant step forward in benchmarking ML automation, but it is not a groundbreaking paradigm shift. The individual components (web scraping, leaderboard analysis, multi-metric evaluation) are not entirely new, but their integration within a single benchmark, specifically tailored for LLM-based ML agents, adds value. The practicality of the "Lite" version and scalability enabled by automation are key strengths. However, the limitations stemming from the reliance on existing competition data need to be considered. It is a well-engineered and timely benchmark with the potential to influence the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### OmniEVA: Embodied Versatile Planner via Task-Adaptive 3D-Grounded and Embodiment-aware Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09332v1)
- **Authors**: Yuecheng Liu, Dafeng Chi, Shiguang Wu, Zhanguang Zhang, Yuzheng Zhuang, Bowen Yang, He Zhu, Lingfeng Zhang, Pengwei Xie, David Gamaliel Arcos Bravo, Yingxue Zhang, Jianye Hao, Xingyue Quan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have opened new opportunities for embodied intelligence, enabling multimodal understanding, reasoning, and interaction, as well as continuous spatial decision-making. Nevertheless, current MLLM-based embodied systems face two critical limitations. First, Geometric Adaptability Gap: models trained solely on 2D inputs or with hard-coded 3D geometry injection suffer from either insufficient spatial information or restricted 2D generalization, leading to poor adaptability across tasks with diverse spatial demands. Second, Embodiment Constraint Gap: prior work often neglects the physical constraints and capacities of real robots, resulting in task plans that are theoretically valid but practically infeasible.To address these gaps, we introduce OmniEVA -- an embodied versatile planner that enables advanced embodied reasoning and task planning through two pivotal innovations: (1) a Task-Adaptive 3D Grounding mechanism, which introduces a gated router to perform explicit selective regulation of 3D fusion based on contextual requirements, enabling context-aware 3D grounding for diverse embodied tasks. (2) an Embodiment-Aware Reasoning framework that jointly incorporates task goals and embodiment constraints into the reasoning loop, resulting in planning decisions that are both goal-directed and executable. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that OmniEVA not only achieves state-of-the-art general embodied reasoning performance, but also exhibits a strong ability across a wide range of downstream scenarios. Evaluations of a suite of proposed embodied benchmarks, including both primitive and composite tasks, confirm its robust and versatile planning capabilities. Project page: https://omnieva.github.io
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OmniEVA: Embodied Versatile Planner via Task-Adaptive 3D-Grounded and Embodiment-Aware Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OmniEVA, a new embodied versatile planner designed to improve reasoning and task planning for robots.  OmniEVA addresses two key limitations of existing MLLM-based embodied systems: the *Geometric Adaptability Gap* (poor performance across tasks with varying spatial demands) and the *Embodiment Constraint Gap* (neglecting physical constraints of real robots).  The core innovations include:

1.  **Task-Adaptive 3D Grounding:** A gated router selectively integrates 3D features based on contextual requirements, enabling context-aware 3D grounding.
2.  **Embodiment-Aware Reasoning:** A framework that incorporates task goals and robot embodiment constraints (affordances, workspace, kinematics) into the reasoning loop, leading to more executable plans.

The authors demonstrate OmniEVA's capabilities on a range of embodied reasoning benchmarks, showing state-of-the-art performance and robust planning abilities across diverse scenarios.  They introduce new benchmarks focusing on primitive embodied tasks to specifically evaluate embodiment-aware planning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's core contribution lies in the *task-adaptive* 3D grounding and the *embodiment-aware* reasoning framework.  While 3D-LLMs exist, the dynamic feature selection for 3D grounding based on task context is a novel and sensible approach. Similarly, many reinforcement learning algorithms incorporate physical constraints, the explicit integration of those constraint factors during the LLM's reasoning process (guided by the "think" format) provides more human interpretable and correct solution, is a notable step. The introduction of the new embodied benchmarks is also a valuable addition, addressing the lack of such resources. The qualitative results showcase the capability of the model with embodiment-aware training.

*   **Significance:** The identified limitations in current MLLM-based embodied systems are critical bottlenecks for real-world deployment. By addressing these gaps, OmniEVA makes a significant contribution towards building more versatile and practical robots. The improvement in robotic task performance is crucial, but is also very dependant on the low level controller, which would need to be optimized for each hardware or environment. Also, the lack of detail on the physical robot being used, would have been helpful (e.g. robot arm used is an ABB IRB 1200-5/0.9 etc.).

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem formulation is clear and well-motivated.
    *   The proposed architecture and training methodology are well-described.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, demonstrating the effectiveness of OmniEVA across a range of benchmarks.
    *   The introduction of new primitive embodied benchmarks is a valuable contribution.
