The paper "Prompt Pirates Need a Map: Stealing Seeds helps Stealing Prompts" addresses the security and privacy concerns surrounding prompt stealing in diffusion models for image generation. The authors demonstrate that existing prompt recovery methods are fundamentally limited because they ignore the impact of the initial random noise seed used for image generation. They identify a noise generation vulnerability (CWE-339) in prevalent image generation frameworks where the seed values are capped at 32 bits. This limitation allows an attacker to brute-force the seed within a relatively short period. Using a seed-recovery tool called *SeedSnitch*, they demonstrate that approximately 95% of seed values from the popular platform CivitAI can be brute-forced within 140 minutes. Leveraging the recovered seed, they propose *PromptPirate*, a genetic algorithm-based optimization method for prompt stealing, which outperforms existing methods. Finally, they propose countermeasures to mitigate seed stealing and initiated responsible disclosure with developers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its recognition and exploitation of the importance of the initial seed and the CWE-339 vulnerability related to seed generation in diffusion models. While prior work acknowledged the importance of seeds in diffusion models, this paper is the first to explicitly identify and exploit the seed-generation vulnerability to enhance prompt stealing. The use of a genetic algorithm, PromptPirate, is not entirely novel as optimization techniques have been used, but its specific application to prompt stealing *given the seed* is well-articulated and shows improvement over prior work.

*   **Significance:**  The paper highlights a significant security and privacy risk. Prompt stealing represents a tangible threat to artists, designers, and businesses who invest heavily in crafting effective prompts. By demonstrating the feasibility of seed recovery and subsequent prompt reconstruction, the paper exposes a critical vulnerability in commonly used image generation pipelines. The paper takes a proactive approach by offering practical mitigation strategies. This effort to engage with developers is valuable and enhances the impact of the research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined threat model.
    *   Thorough empirical evaluation using real-world data (CivitAI) and comparisons to state-of-the-art methods.
    *   Practical and actionable mitigation strategies proposed.
    *   Responsible disclosure process initiated with developers.
    *   Open-source release of tools (SeedSnitch and PromptPirate) for further security research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The core vulnerability (CWE-339 due to truncated seeds) is a known weakness in PRNG implementations. The novelty stems from its application to diffusion models rather than its discovery.
    *   The mitigation strategies, although effective, might be seen as "low-hanging fruit." More robust, perhaps hardware-based, random number generation techniques might be desirable in the long term.
    *   The study focuses heavily on one platform (CivitAI) and one class of diffusion models (Stable Diffusion). While this is justifiable for a focused study, the generalizability of the findings to other platforms and model architectures needs further investigation.
    *   While the paper presents performance improvements with PromptPirate, the qualitative results indicate that PromptStealer might achieve higher semantic similarity. More nuance in the evaluation could explore the trade-off between semantic richness and visual fidelity of the recovered prompts.

*   **Impact:**  The paper is likely to have a tangible impact on the security practices of diffusion model developers and users. It is a crucial reminder that seemingly minor details like the random seed can have significant security implications. This research will likely prompt increased focus on robust random number generation techniques and could lead to enhanced security measures in commercial image generation platforms.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5**. The paper presents a well-executed study that exposes a real and present security vulnerability in widely-used diffusion models. The identification and exploitation of the seed-generation vulnerability is novel in the context of prompt stealing, and the proposed mitigation strategies are practical and useful. However, the reliance on a somewhat known CWE-339 vulnerability and the limited scope of the evaluation prevent it from reaching a higher score.  The responsible disclosure and open-source contributions further enhance its value and overall impact on the community. The paper is a valuable contribution to the emerging field of diffusion model security.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mixture of Semantics Transmission for Generative AI-Enabled Semantic Communication Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09499v1)
- **Authors**: Junjie Ni, Tong Wu, Zhiyong Chen, Yin Xu, Meixia Tao, Wenjun Zhang
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a mixture of semantics (MoS) transmission strategy for wireless semantic communication systems based on generative artificial intelligence (AI). At the transmitter, we divide an image into regions of interest (ROI) and reigons of non-interest (RONI) to extract their semantic information respectively. Semantic information of ROI can be allocated more bandwidth, while RONI can be represented in a compact form for transmission. At the receiver, a diffusion model reconstructs the full image using the received semantic information of ROI and RONI. Compared to existing generative AI-based methods, MoS enables more efficient use of channel resources by balancing visual fidelity and semantic relevance. Experimental results demonstrate that appropriate ROI-RONI allocation is critical. The MoS achieves notable performance gains in peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) of ROI and CLIP score of RONI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "Mixture of Semantics" (MoS) transmission strategy for wireless semantic communication systems, leveraging generative AI. The approach divides an image into Region of Interest (ROI) and Region of Non-Interest (RONI), allocating more bandwidth to accurately transmit the ROI while using a lightweight textual description for the RONI. A diffusion model at the receiver reconstructs the full image using both the received ROI data and the semantic information from the RONI. Experimental results show that appropriate ROI-RONI allocation improves performance (PSNR for ROI, CLIP score for RONI) compared to existing generative AI-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in the application of ROI/RONI segmentation to the generative AI-based semantic communication framework. While ROI-based approaches exist in traditional image compression and earlier JSCC methods, the *combination* with generative AI (specifically diffusion models and vision-language models like BLIP) *and* the explicit handling of RONI using semantic descriptions makes this a novel contribution.  The strategy of using different semantic modalities for ROI and RONI transmission addresses the limitation of earlier methods that focused solely on pixel-level consistency or global semantic similarity.

**Significance:**

*   **Efficient Resource Utilization:**  The key contribution is the potential for more efficient utilization of channel resources. By allocating bandwidth dynamically based on semantic importance (ROI vs. RONI), the MoS approach promises improved overall image reconstruction quality under bandwidth constraints.
*   **Increased Flexibility:** The pixel-level specification of ROI allows users to prioritize specific image regions for high-fidelity transmission, which increases the system's flexibility to meet diverse user needs.
*   **Combines Strengths of Different Modalities:** The strategy synergistically combines pixel-accurate transmission for regions needing fine detail with semantic-level descriptions for context. This addresses a clear gap in prior AI-based image transmission approaches.
*   **Addresses Perceptual Fidelity and Control:** It provides improved perceptual fidelity and a higher degree of similarity compared to generating an entirely new image. This is because it allows for increased controllability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on SAM:** The performance depends heavily on the segmentation model (SAM). SAM, while powerful, can still make errors, especially with complex scenes or ambiguous prompts. These errors will propagate through the rest of the system, impacting reconstruction quality. The paper doesn't thoroughly analyze the impact of segmentation errors.
*   **Complexity:** The system introduces added complexity with SAM, BLIP, and the diffusion model. The computational overhead and latency could be significant barriers to real-time applications.
*   **Parameter Tuning:** The crucial parameter 'a' (symbol allocation to text) is determined empirically.  A more principled approach for dynamically adapting 'a' based on channel conditions, image content, and user preferences would be beneficial.
*   **Limited Comparative Analysis:** The comparison is performed with a relatively limited set of baselines. While SwinJSCC and DiffCom are relevant, comparing against more recent state-of-the-art JSCC methods and adaptive bandwidth allocation techniques would strengthen the paper.
*   **Dataset Specificity:** Experiments primarily focus on the RefCOCO dataset. The performance on a more diverse range of image datasets (including those with more complex scenes, varying object sizes, and different types of semantic content) would be valuable.
*   **CLIP-RONI Weakness:** The authors admit that MoS strategy performs worse on CLIP-RONI because DiffCom allocates more resources for transmitting the RONI.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper provides a promising new direction for wireless image semantic communication. The concept of integrating segmentation with generative AI and modality selection is potentially impactful. The MoS strategy could inspire further research into adaptive bandwidth allocation, optimized ROI/RONI segmentation techniques, and the use of alternative vision-language models. The study emphasizes a need for a balanced approach between pixel and semantic fidelity.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid piece of research that introduces a novel and practically relevant approach to image semantic communication. It addresses limitations of existing methods by effectively leveraging generative AI and modality selection. However, the paper's value is somewhat limited by its reliance on SAM, the empirical nature of 'a' parameter selection, limited baselines, dataset scope and weakness in CLIP-RONI. The method has the potential to influence future research directions, but its practicality is also limited by complexity.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IT
- **Score**: 7/10

### DeMeVa at LeWiDi-2025: Modeling Perspectives with In-Context Learning and Label Distribution Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09524v1)
- **Authors**: Daniil Ignatev, Nan Li, Hugh Mee Wong, Anh Dang, Shane Kaszefski Yaschuk
- **Abstract**: This system paper presents the DeMeVa team's approaches to the third edition of the Learning with Disagreements shared task (LeWiDi 2025; Leonardelli et al., 2025). We explore two directions: in-context learning (ICL) with large language models, where we compare example sampling strategies; and label distribution learning (LDL) methods with RoBERTa (Liu et al., 2019b), where we evaluate several fine-tuning methods. Our contributions are twofold: (1) we show that ICL can effectively predict annotator-specific annotations (perspectivist annotations), and that aggregating these predictions into soft labels yields competitive performance; and (2) we argue that LDL methods are promising for soft label predictions and merit further exploration by the perspectivist community.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper describes the DeMeVa team's contributions to the LeWiDi 2025 shared task, focusing on learning from disagreements in annotations. They explore two main approaches: (1) In-Context Learning (ICL) using large language models, testing different example selection strategies, and (2) Label Distribution Learning (LDL) using RoBERTa with various fine-tuning methods. They demonstrate that ICL can predict annotator-specific labels and that aggregating these predictions performs well. They also suggest that LDL methods hold promise for soft label prediction in perspectivist settings and warrant further investigation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This paper presents a reasonable contribution to the Learning with Disagreements field, but its novelty and significance are somewhat limited.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Relevant Problem:** Learning from annotator disagreements is a crucial challenge in natural language processing. The LeWiDi shared task provides a valuable benchmark for this problem.
*   **Exploration of Two Different Approaches:** The paper investigates both ICL and LDL, which offers a broader perspective on potential solutions. Comparing these two fundamentally different methods is valuable.
*   **Focus on Perspectivist Annotations:** Explicitly addressing annotator-specific (perspectivist) annotations is a positive aspect, as it aligns with the nuances of disagreement.
*   **Positive Empirical Results (implied):** The abstract mentions "competitive performance" using aggregated ICL predictions, suggesting successful empirical results. However, the level of performance relative to other systems remains unclear.
*   **Clear Direction for Future Research:** The paper concludes with a call for further research into LDL methods, which is a reasonable suggestion based on their findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While exploring ICL and LDL is worthwhile, the application of these methods to the LeWiDi task is not inherently novel. The specific sampling strategies within ICL and the fine-tuning methods within LDL are where the potential novelty lies. However, the abstract lacks specific details about these variations, making it difficult to assess their innovativeness. Without this detail, it risks being a "try everything" approach.
*   **Limited Depth of Exploration:** The abstract provides a high-level overview but lacks detail about the specific implementation of ICL and LDL. Questions that arise: What large language models were used? What sampling strategies were tested? What fine-tuning methods were applied to RoBERTa? How were the soft labels aggregated? Addressing these questions would improve the evaluation.
*   **Lack of Concrete Results in the Abstract:** The abstract mentions "competitive performance" but lacks specific metrics or comparisons to baselines or other systems. This weakens the impact of the findings. A more quantitative abstract would have a larger impact.
*   **Unclear Scope of Impact:** While suggesting future research directions is good, the impact of the paper is dependent on the success of the full paper in explaining the approaches in detail, presenting clear and rigorous results, and discussing limitations.
*   **Potential for Redundancy:** It is unclear if these approaches have already been widely applied in the context of similar shared tasks. If so, the novelty would be further diminished. The novelty rests in the combination of ICL and LDL *specifically* for perspectivist annotations, and the abstract must sell that aspect.

**Justification:**

The paper addresses a relevant and important problem, and the exploration of two distinct approaches is valuable. However, the limited detail in the abstract regarding the specific methods and empirical results makes it difficult to assess the true novelty and significance of the work. The paper falls short of pushing the field significantly forward, but provides some new insights and suggests a promising direction for future research. Without more information regarding the precise innovation involved in the sampling and fine-tuning methods, and the actual empirical performance, it's difficult to argue for a high score. The potential for redundancy with existing approaches to similar tasks also limits the score.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Prompting the Market? A Large-Scale Meta-Analysis of GenAI in Finance NLP (2022-2025)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09544v1)
- **Authors**: Paolo Pedinotti, Peter Baumann, Nathan Jessurun, Leslie Barrett, Enrico Santus
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have rapidly reshaped financial NLP, enabling new tasks and driving a proliferation of datasets and diversification of data sources. Yet, this transformation has outpaced traditional surveys. In this paper, we present MetaGraph, a generalizable methodology for extracting knowledge graphs from scientific literature and analyzing them to obtain a structured, queryable view of research trends. We define an ontology for financial NLP research and apply an LLM-based extraction pipeline to 681 papers (2022-2025), enabling large-scale, data-driven analysis. MetaGraph reveals three key phases: early LLM adoption and task/dataset innovation; critical reflection on LLM limitations; and growing integration of peripheral techniques into modular systems. This structured view offers both practitioners and researchers a clear understanding of how financial NLP has evolved - highlighting emerging trends, shifting priorities, and methodological shifts-while also demonstrating a reusable approach for mapping scientific progress in other domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MetaGraph, a methodology employing LLMs for automated knowledge graph construction from scientific literature, specifically focusing on financial NLP research papers from 2022-2025. The approach involves defining an ontology for financial NLP, using LLMs in a human-in-the-loop manner to extract relevant information, and building a queryable knowledge graph. The application of MetaGraph to 681 papers reveals key trends in the field, including early LLM adoption, reflections on LLM limitations, and the integration of peripheral technologies. The authors provide a generalizable pipeline for extracting quantitative insights, map field evolution, and share their constructed knowledge graph.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of LLM-based knowledge graph construction to analyze trends in a specific subfield of NLP – financial NLP.  Existing surveys rely on more traditional, narrative methods. MetaGraph's automated approach allows for quantitative analysis of a large corpus. The paper's novelty also resides in the detailed findings about the shift in Financial NLP after the introduction of ChatGPT, including the growing dominance of financial QA, the increased use of synthetic datasets, and the shift from model-centric to system-oriented design. However, some existing works have already explored using LLMs for knowledge graph construction, so the methodological novelty might be incremental.

*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from its ability to provide a structured, data-driven understanding of the rapid changes occurring in financial NLP due to the advent of LLMs. The findings offer valuable insights for both practitioners and researchers, highlighting emerging trends, shifting priorities, and methodological shifts. The MetaGraph methodology itself is a significant contribution, offering a reusable framework for mapping scientific progress in other domains.
    *  *Strengths:* The use of a large dataset (681 papers) lends credibility to the findings. The defined ontology and the human-in-the-loop approach to LLM extraction contribute to the accuracy of the knowledge graph. The paper is well-structured and clearly presents its findings.
    *   *Weaknesses:* The study heavily relies on LLM capabilities for information extraction. While the paper mentions a human-in-the-loop approach, the potential for LLM biases and inaccuracies remains a concern.  The manual definition of the ontology, while providing structure, also introduces an inductive bias. The analysis, while quantitative, is based on extracted data, and the interpretation of these extractions is still subject to human judgment. The generalizability of specific findings about the financial NLP domain to other fields might be limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** MetaGraph has the potential to become a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners in various fields. The knowledge graph and methodology provide a systematic way to track research trends, identify emerging technologies, and understand the impact of new tools and techniques. The insights from the financial NLP analysis can inform strategies for LLM adoption and development in other domains. The framework could also be extended and adapted to analyze other forms of unstructured data such as patents, news articles, or market reports.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5** due to the following reasons:

*   The paper tackles a relevant problem by providing a systematic way to understand the impact of LLMs on a rapidly evolving field.
*   The MetaGraph methodology is a valuable contribution that can be applied to other domains.
*   The findings about the specific trends in financial NLP are significant and provide actionable insights.
*   While the paper does not invent entirely novel LLM techniques, it provides a valuable application of existing tools and a robust methodology for knowledge graph construction.

The limitations relating to potential LLM biases and reliance on human interpretation slightly reduce the score, especially concerning the reliability of the extracted and generated knowledge graph. While the methodology may be generally applicable, many of the results are specific to the financial domain, limiting the overall impact.

### Improving Video Diffusion Transformer Training by Multi-Feature Fusion and Alignment from Self-Supervised Vision Encoders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09547v1)
- **Authors**: Dohun Lee, Hyeonho Jeong, Jiwook Kim, Duygu Ceylan, Jong Chul Ye
- **Abstract**: Video diffusion models have advanced rapidly in the recent years as a result of series of architectural innovations (e.g., diffusion transformers) and use of novel training objectives (e.g., flow matching). In contrast, less attention has been paid to improving the feature representation power of such models. In this work, we show that training video diffusion models can benefit from aligning the intermediate features of the video generator with feature representations of pre-trained vision encoders. We propose a new metric and conduct an in-depth analysis of various vision encoders to evaluate their discriminability and temporal consistency, thereby assessing their suitability for video feature alignment. Based on the analysis, we present Align4Gen which provides a novel multi-feature fusion and alignment method integrated into video diffusion model training. We evaluate Align4Gen both for unconditional and class-conditional video generation tasks and show that it results in improved video generation as quantified by various metrics. Full video results are available on our project page: https://align4gen.github.io/align4gen/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Improving Video Diffusion Transformer Training by Multi-Feature Fusion and Alignment from Self-Supervised Vision Encoders" introduces Align4Gen, a framework designed to improve video diffusion model training by aligning intermediate features of the video generator with feature representations from pre-trained vision encoders. The authors propose a new metric, Intra-Inter Consistency Ratio (IICR), to evaluate the suitability of different vision encoders for this alignment.  They find that image-trained encoders often exhibit superior discriminability and temporal consistency compared to video-trained encoders, and that different image features (DINOv2 and SAM2.1 Hiera) capture complementary frequency signals.  Align4Gen fuses features from multiple pre-trained image encoders to guide video diffusion transformer training.  The authors demonstrate improved video generation quality on unconditional and class-conditional video generation tasks, as quantified by various metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach to improve video diffusion model training by leveraging pre-trained vision encoders and aligning their features with the video generator's intermediate features. The IICR metric is a useful tool for analyzing the properties of vision encoders and selecting the most suitable ones for feature alignment. The multi-feature fusion strategy is also novel, as it combines features from different pre-trained models to capture complementary information. The idea of using external pre-trained vision encoders as a guide for feature learning within video diffusion models is inspired by REPA, but this paper extends the idea to video and presents the IICR metric for analysis, thus adding its own value.

*   **Significance:** The paper's results demonstrate that Align4Gen can significantly improve video generation quality and accelerate training. This is a significant contribution to the field, as it addresses the important problem of improving the feature representation power of video diffusion models. The paper also provides insights into the properties of different vision encoders and their suitability for feature alignment, which can guide future research in this area. The demonstration of achieving similar results in fewer steps (reduced computational cost) makes this a valuable contribution. The method seems fairly generic and could be used to improve a variety of diffusion model architectures.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper clearly identifies the need to improve the feature representation power of video diffusion models.
    *   **Novel approach:** Align4Gen provides a novel framework for improving video diffusion model training.
    *   **Quantitative and qualitative results:** The paper presents both quantitative and qualitative results to support its claims.
    *   **Thorough analysis:** The paper includes a thorough analysis of different vision encoders and their properties.
    *   **Well-written and easy to understand:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited dataset diversity:** The paper's experiments are primarily conducted on the UCF-101 dataset, which may not be representative of all video generation tasks. Expanding to other datasets like Kinetics would further strengthen the claims.
    *   **Ablation studies could be expanded:** While the ablation studies are thorough, adding more detailed analysis regarding the selection of alignment layer depths could further enhance the study.
    *   **Reliance on specific encoders:** The method's effectiveness is tied to the performance of pre-trained encoders like DINOv2 and SAM2.1 Hiera. The long-term viability depends on the evolution of such pre-trained models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in video diffusion models by highlighting the importance of feature representation power and providing a practical framework for improving it. The IICR metric may also be adopted by other researchers to evaluate vision encoders. The acceleration in training and improved results are also likely to make the work influential.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of video diffusion models.  The Align4Gen framework effectively leverages pre-trained vision encoders to improve video generation quality and accelerate training. While there are some limitations, such as the dataset diversity and reliance on specific encoders, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The thorough analysis, clear presentation, and promising results make this a valuable contribution that is likely to inspire future research in this area.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Finite Scalar Quantization Enables Redundant and Transmission-Robust Neural Audio Compression at Low Bit-rates
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09550v1)
- **Authors**: Harry Julia, Rachel Beeson, Lohith Konathala, Johanna Ulin, Jiameng Gao
- **Abstract**: Neural Audio Codecs (NACs) have become increasingly adopted in speech processing tasks due to their excellent rate-distortion performance and compatibility with Large Language Models (LLMs) as discrete feature representations for audio generation. While most existing codecs rely on Residual Vector Quantization (RVQ), Finite Scalar Quantization (FSQ) has recently emerged as a compelling alternative that simplifies training and natively supports single codebooks. We introduce NeuCodec, an FSQ-based NAC, and show that FSQ encodes baked-in redundancy which produces an encoding which is robust when transmitted through noisy channels. First, through an encoder distillation experiment, we show that two different encoders can learn to encode identical audio into vastly different code sequences whilst maintaining comparable reconstruction quality with the same quantizer and decoder. Second, we demonstrate that FSQ has vastly superior bit-level perturbation robustness by comparing the performance of RVQ and FSQ codecs when simulating the transmission of code sequences through a noisy channel.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces NeuCodec, a neural audio codec based on Finite Scalar Quantization (FSQ). The core contribution is demonstrating that FSQ-based codecs exhibit inherent redundancy, leading to improved robustness against transmission errors compared to codecs relying on Residual Vector Quantization (RVQ). The authors demonstrate this through: 1) Encoder distillation experiments showing that different encoders can produce vastly different code sequences for the same audio while maintaining comparable reconstruction quality; and 2) Simulating noisy channel transmission, revealing that FSQ codecs degrade more gracefully than RVQ codecs under bit-level perturbations.  The authors suggest that this robustness arises from the inherent distribution of information across codewords in FSQ, creating local redundancy and a locality in representation space.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of FSQ to neural audio codecs is not entirely novel, as prior work like StableCodec also utilizes FSQ. However, the *explicit* focus on, and demonstration of, the *redundancy* and *noise robustness* properties of FSQ in this context *is* a significant contribution. The encoder distillation experiments are a clever approach to illustrating the inherent flexibility in FSQ-based encodings. The bit-level perturbation experiment provides strong empirical evidence for the improved robustness.

*   **Significance:**  The improved robustness against bit-level perturbations is particularly important for applications where audio is transmitted over unreliable channels, such as mobile networks or the internet. This work suggests a potential advantage of FSQ over RVQ that could lead to more practical and resilient audio communication systems. The observation of inherent redundancy opens interesting avenues for exploring error correction and concealment techniques directly within the codec design. The results are also relevant to the development of more robust speech/audio representations for downstream tasks like ASR.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-structured presentation.
    *   Well-designed experiments to support the claims.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation metrics.
    *   The encoder distillation experiment offers a novel perspective on FSQ representations.
    *   The perturbation experiment clearly highlights the advantage of FSQ in noisy environments.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of why FSQ leads to this redundancy, beyond the intuitive explanation provided.  A more rigorous information-theoretic analysis could strengthen the conclusions.
    *   While the paper mentions potential applications, it lacks concrete examples or implementations of utilizing the observed robustness in a real-world system. Demonstrating this would significantly increase the impact.
    *   The comparison only includes a few codecs, and expanding this to include other types of vector quantization or alternative quantization methods would enhance the robustness of the conclusions.
    *   The paper could compare the model size and encoding/decoding complexities of NeuCodec with other codecs to have a fair comparison and understand the computational overhead.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This work has the potential to influence the design of future low-bitrate audio codecs, particularly those targeted for noisy or unreliable communication channels. It could also spur further research into techniques for exploiting the inherent redundancy of FSQ for error correction or concealment. The insights from this work could be applicable to other domains beyond audio, such as image or video compression.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating the advantages of FSQ in the context of neural audio codecs. The experimental results are compelling and the insights are relevant to real-world applications. While the theoretical analysis could be strengthened, and the practical applications are not fully explored, the paper presents a novel and significant finding.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fluent but Unfeeling: The Emotional Blind Spots of Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09593v1)
- **Authors**: Bangzhao Shu, Isha Joshi, Melissa Karnaze, Anh C. Pham, Ishita Kakkar, Sindhu Kothe, Arpine Hovasapian, Mai ElSherief
- **Abstract**: The versatility of Large Language Models (LLMs) in natural language understanding has made them increasingly popular in mental health research. While many studies explore LLMs' capabilities in emotion recognition, a critical gap remains in evaluating whether LLMs align with human emotions at a fine-grained level. Existing research typically focuses on classifying emotions into predefined, limited categories, overlooking more nuanced expressions. To address this gap, we introduce EXPRESS, a benchmark dataset curated from Reddit communities featuring 251 fine-grained, self-disclosed emotion labels. Our comprehensive evaluation framework examines predicted emotion terms and decomposes them into eight basic emotions using established emotion theories, enabling a fine-grained comparison. Systematic testing of prevalent LLMs under various prompt settings reveals that accurately predicting emotions that align with human self-disclosed emotions remains challenging. Qualitative analysis further shows that while certain LLMs generate emotion terms consistent with established emotion theories and definitions, they sometimes fail to capture contextual cues as effectively as human self-disclosures. These findings highlight the limitations of LLMs in fine-grained emotion alignment and offer insights for future research aimed at enhancing their contextual understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current LLM emotion recognition research, which often relies on coarse-grained emotion categories. The authors introduce EXPRESS, a new benchmark dataset with 251 fine-grained, self-disclosed emotion labels from Reddit. They evaluate several LLMs using this dataset and a framework that decomposes predicted emotions into eight basic emotions. The results show LLMs struggle to accurately predict and align with human self-disclosed emotions at a fine-grained level, often missing contextual cues. The paper highlights the need for improved contextual understanding in LLMs for accurate emotion recognition in mental health applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a significant gap in LLM emotion recognition research: the lack of fine-grained evaluation. The creation of the EXPRESS dataset is a strength, offering a more realistic and nuanced representation of human emotions than typically found in benchmark datasets. The decomposition framework based on established emotion theories provides a solid methodological approach for analyzing LLM predictions. The systematic testing of various LLMs under different prompting conditions is also commendable, as it allows for a more comprehensive understanding of their capabilities and limitations.

However, the paper also has potential weaknesses. While the dataset is a valuable contribution, its creation solely from Reddit posts may introduce biases reflecting the demographics and culture of Reddit users. The generalization of findings to other populations and contexts should be considered.

The paper's novelty lies in its focus on fine-grained emotion recognition and the introduction of the EXPRESS dataset. While previous work has explored emotion recognition using LLMs, the granularity of emotion labels and the evaluation framework presented here are relatively novel.

The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the accuracy and reliability of LLMs in mental health applications. Accurate emotion recognition is crucial for tasks such as mental health support and personalized interventions. By identifying the limitations of current LLMs, this paper can guide future research toward developing more effective and context-aware models. The EXPRESS dataset can serve as a valuable resource for researchers working on emotion recognition and natural language understanding.

However, the study's focus solely on Reddit data could limit the generalizability of findings. Furthermore, while the paper acknowledges the importance of contextual understanding, it doesn't propose concrete solutions for improving this aspect in LLMs.

Despite these limitations, the paper's contribution to the field is substantial. It highlights a critical gap in LLM emotion recognition and provides a valuable resource (the EXPRESS dataset) and evaluation framework for future research. The findings have implications for the development of more reliable and accurate LLMs for mental health applications. The identification of context as a key challenge is also important, setting the stage for future investigations into improving contextual understanding in LLMs.

Considering the novelty of the fine-grained approach, the value of the dataset and evaluation framework, and the potential impact on mental health applications, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### How much are LLMs changing the language of academic papers after ChatGPT? A multi-database and full text analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09596v1)
- **Authors**: Kayvan Kousha, Mike Thelwall
- **Abstract**: This study investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) are influencing the language of academic papers by tracking 12 LLM-associated terms across six major scholarly databases (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, PubMed Central (PMC), Dimensions, and OpenAlex) from 2015 to 2024. Using over 2.4 million PMC open-access publications (2021-July 2025), we also analysed full texts to assess changes in the frequency and co-occurrence of these terms before and after ChatGPT's initial public release. Across databases, delve (+1,500%), underscore (+1,000%), and intricate (+700%) had the largest increases between 2022 and 2024. Growth in LLM-term usage was much higher in STEM fields than in social sciences and arts and humanities. In PMC full texts, the proportion of papers using underscore six or more times increased by over 10,000% from 2022 to 2025, followed by intricate (+5,400%) and meticulous (+2,800%). Nearly half of all 2024 PMC papers using any LLM term also included underscore, compared with only 3%-14% of papers before ChatGPT in 2022. Papers using one LLM term are now much more likely to include other terms. For example, in 2024, underscore strongly correlated with pivotal (0.449) and delve (0.311), compared with very weak associations in 2022 (0.032 and 0.018, respectively). These findings provide the first large-scale evidence based on full-text publications and multiple databases that some LLM-related terms are now being used much more frequently and together. The rapid uptake of LLMs to support scholarly publishing is a welcome development reducing the language barrier to academic publishing for non-English speakers.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This study investigates the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT on the language used in academic papers. By analyzing a large dataset of academic publications from multiple databases (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, PMC, Dimensions, and OpenAlex) between 2015 and 2024, the authors tracked the frequency and co-occurrence of 12 LLM-associated terms. The study finds a significant increase in the usage of these terms after the release of ChatGPT, particularly in STEM fields. The terms 'delve' and 'underscore' showed the most substantial growth. The co-occurrence of LLM-related terms within the same papers also increased significantly. Furthermore, correlation analysis revealed that these terms became more closely related after ChatGPT's release, indicating a shift in academic writing style influenced by LLMs. The study also explores the usage of LLM-associated terms in retracted articles and discusses the limitations of their analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The study provides a comprehensive, large-scale analysis of LLM-related term usage in academic papers across multiple databases. This full-text analysis expands upon earlier studies that were often limited to abstracts, specific fields, or shorter timeframes.  The explicit analysis of term *co-occurrence* patterns and correlations is more innovative than simply tracking term frequency. However, the chosen terms could be argued to be not perfectly "LLM-related". Thus, the authors' hypothesis relies on an assumption, weakening the findings.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant as they offer empirical evidence of how LLMs are changing academic writing. The identified trends may reflect a shift towards more standardized, possibly less nuanced language. The higher usage of LLM-associated terms in STEM fields compared to social sciences and humanities suggests disciplinary differences in LLM adoption and influence. The analysis of retracted articles raises concerns about the quality of papers relying on LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Large dataset and multiple databases enhance the robustness of the findings.
    *   Full-text analysis provides a more detailed understanding of LLM influence than abstract-only studies.
    *   Exploration of co-occurrence patterns and correlations adds valuable insights.
    *   Analysis of retracted articles is a unique and important contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study relies on the assumption that the increased use of selected terms is directly caused by LLMs, without directly proving this causal link.
    *   Selection bias in the terms chosen may influence results.
    *   The analysis is limited to English-language publications and may not represent global trends.
    *   The PMC full-text dataset is limited to open-access articles, potentially skewing results.
    *   Lacks an investigation of qualitative semantic changes in language, just frequency changes.

*   **Impact:** The paper's findings have implications for understanding the evolving landscape of academic writing in the age of AI. It raises important questions about the impact of LLMs on language quality, originality, and potential biases. The findings could inform discussions about ethical guidelines for LLM use in academic research and publishing.

**Overall:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive analysis of LLM-related term usage in academic publications. While the study has some limitations, the large dataset, multiple databases, and unique analysis of co-occurrence patterns and retracted articles enhance its significance. The identified trends and insights will be of interest to researchers, publishers, and policymakers concerned with the future of academic writing in the age of AI.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.DL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LAVA: Language Model Assisted Verbal Autopsy for Cause-of-Death Determination
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09602v1)
- **Authors**: Yiqun T. Chen, Tyler H. McCormick, Li Liu, Abhirup Datta
- **Abstract**: Verbal autopsy (VA) is a critical tool for estimating causes of death in resource-limited settings where medical certification is unavailable. This study presents LA-VA, a proof-of-concept pipeline that combines Large Language Models (LLMs) with traditional algorithmic approaches and embedding-based classification for improved cause-of-death prediction. Using the Population Health Metrics Research Consortium (PHMRC) dataset across three age categories (Adult: 7,580; Child: 1,960; Neonate: 2,438), we evaluate multiple approaches: GPT-5 predictions, LCVA baseline, text embeddings, and meta-learner ensembles. Our results demonstrate that GPT-5 achieves the highest individual performance with average test site accuracies of 48.6% (Adult), 50.5% (Child), and 53.5% (Neonate), outperforming traditional statistical machine learning baselines by 5-10%. Our findings suggest that simple off-the-shelf LLM-assisted approaches could substantially improve verbal autopsy accuracy, with important implications for global health surveillance in low-resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LAVA: Language Model Assisted Verbal Autopsy for Cause-of-Death Determination":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LA-VA, a system that combines Large Language Models (LLMs) with traditional machine learning approaches and embeddings to improve cause-of-death (COD) prediction from verbal autopsy (VA) data.  The authors leverage the PHMRC dataset and evaluate GPT-5, LCVA, text embeddings, and meta-learner ensembles across adult, child, and neonate age groups using a leave-one-site-out (LOSO) cross-validation approach. The results demonstrate that GPT-5, particularly after post-hoc calibration, achieves the highest individual performance, outperforming traditional methods.  The work highlights the potential of LLMs to enhance global health surveillance in resource-constrained settings by improving VA accuracy and addressing issues like distributional shifts and free-text analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of large language models to the verbal autopsy domain is a significant step forward. While LLMs are being explored in various medical contexts, their use in VA, specifically addressing the inherent challenges of this domain (narrative text processing, domain shift, label space size), is novel. The exploration of using LLMs directly, in conjunction with existing methods such as latent class VA and embedding-based methods adds to the novelty of the work.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of improved VA accuracy is considerable.  More accurate COD estimations are crucial for public health planning, resource allocation, and intervention strategies, especially in low- and middle-income countries.  The paper demonstrates that LLMs can substantially improve individual-level prediction accuracy and, more importantly, population-level CSMF estimates, which are critical for policy decisions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The study performs a thorough evaluation across multiple age groups, sites, and model architectures, including baseline models.  The use of leave-one-site-out (LOSO) cross-validation provides a more realistic assessment of generalizability than standard cross-validation.
    *   **Focus on Practical Challenges:**  The paper directly addresses key challenges in VA, such as domain shift (performance variation across sites), the use of free-text narratives, and calibration of LLM predictions.
    *   **Clear Experimental Design and Metrics:** The paper has a clear description of experiments, and explains their motivations, providing all the details and components used in their LA-VA approach.
    *   **Availability of Code:** Making the code and analysis scripts publicly available enhances reproducibility and facilitates further research in this area.
    *   **Inclusion of Post-hoc calibration**: The use of post-hoc calibration helps improve the population level metrics, which makes the paper relevant to the field of global health.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Historical Data:** The study uses the PHMRC dataset, which was collected from 2007-2010. Newer VA instruments and data collection methods may be used in contemporary field work.
    *   **Computational Requirements:** The use of LLMs, while powerful, presents computational and privacy concerns that may limit deployment in some resource-constrained settings.  The authors acknowledge this limitation, but it is a significant consideration.
    *   **Limited Multilingual Validation:** The paper acknowledges that the experiments are primarily in English, limiting its generalizability to other language settings. Further multilingual validations are essential, given the global nature of VA.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Dependency:** The success of LLM-based approaches heavily relies on prompt engineering. While the paper provides templates, the process of designing optimal prompts for VA is still a challenge and not fully automated. There are methods like automatic prompt engineering, which may allow a better performance for specific populations.
    *   **GPT-5 as a Black Box**: Because of the reliance on a closed-source language model, some concerns regarding reproducibility and interpretability are raised.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could stimulate further research into using LLMs and other machine-learning techniques to improve VA accuracy.
    *   It could lead to the development of more robust and generalizable VA systems that can be deployed in diverse settings.
    *   It highlights the importance of addressing the practical challenges of VA, such as domain shift and free-text narrative processing.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating the potential of LLMs to enhance VA accuracy and address practical challenges in the domain. The comprehensive evaluation and focus on real-world deployment issues are strengths. However, limitations related to reliance on historical data, computational requirements, and a strong dependence on prompt engineering should be taken into consideration. Furthermore, because of the lack of interpretability of the LLM approach and difficulty of replicating results the score does not reach the upper echelons. While there are opportunities for improvement, the work has significant implications for global health surveillance, making it an above-average contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mechanistic Learning with Guided Diffusion Models to Predict Spatio-Temporal Brain Tumor Growth
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09610v1)
- **Authors**: Daria Laslo, Efthymios Georgiou, Marius George Linguraru, Andreas Rauschecker, Sabine Muller, Catherine R. Jutzeler, Sarah Bruningk
- **Abstract**: Predicting the spatio-temporal progression of brain tumors is essential for guiding clinical decisions in neuro-oncology. We propose a hybrid mechanistic learning framework that combines a mathematical tumor growth model with a guided denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM) to synthesize anatomically feasible future MRIs from preceding scans. The mechanistic model, formulated as a system of ordinary differential equations, captures temporal tumor dynamics including radiotherapy effects and estimates future tumor burden. These estimates condition a gradient-guided DDIM, enabling image synthesis that aligns with both predicted growth and patient anatomy. We train our model on the BraTS adult and pediatric glioma datasets and evaluate on 60 axial slices of in-house longitudinal pediatric diffuse midline glioma (DMG) cases. Our framework generates realistic follow-up scans based on spatial similarity metrics. It also introduces tumor growth probability maps, which capture both clinically relevant extent and directionality of tumor growth as shown by 95th percentile Hausdorff Distance. The method enables biologically informed image generation in data-limited scenarios, offering generative-space-time predictions that account for mechanistic priors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel hybrid mechanistic learning framework for predicting the spatio-temporal progression of brain tumors, specifically in data-scarce scenarios like pediatric diffuse midline gliomas (DMG). The framework combines a mathematical tumor growth model (ODE) with a guided denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM). The ODE model captures tumor dynamics and estimates future tumor burden, while the guided DDIM synthesizes anatomically feasible future MRIs conditioned on the ODE's output and the patient's anatomy. The DDIM uses a regressor to predict tumor size and guides the image generation process. The model is trained on BraTS adult and pediatric glioma datasets and evaluated on longitudinal DMG cases, demonstrating realistic follow-up scan generation and clinically relevant tumor growth probability maps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the integration of a mechanistic ODE model with a guided diffusion model for spatio-temporal brain tumor progression prediction. While mechanistic modeling and generative models (specifically DDPMs/DDIMs) have been used separately in medical imaging, the synergistic combination for longitudinal prediction is relatively novel. Specifically, the way the ODE model provides biologically informed constraints for the DDIM, guiding the image generation, is a key contribution.  The use of a guided DDIM conditioned on tumor burden *and* integrated with a tumor growth model is a significant departure from existing work that focuses either on pure generative modeling or pure mechanistic modeling.  The creation of tumor growth probability maps using the bootstrap estimated growth parameters is a useful way to visually summarize uncertainty.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is high, particularly for rare and aggressive tumors like DMG where longitudinal data is scarce. The framework's ability to generate realistic follow-up scans enables biologically informed image generation in data-limited scenarios. This has the potential to improve clinical decision-making, facilitate anticipatory symptom management, and support therapy planning. The ability to create tumor growth probability maps can assist clinicians in understanding the potential extent and directionality of tumor spread, which can be critical for treatment planning. It also offers a potential pathway for synthesizing training data for other machine learning tasks (e.g., segmentation, outcome prediction) when real data is limited.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Hybrid approach:** The integration of mechanistic and generative models provides a robust framework that leverages the strengths of both. The mechanistic model provides biological plausibility and temporal constraints, while the generative model ensures anatomical realism.
    *   **Data-efficient learning:** The framework is designed to address the challenge of data scarcity, making it particularly relevant for rare diseases.
    *   **Clinically relevant outputs:** The generation of tumor growth probability maps provides clinically actionable information that can assist clinicians in decision-making.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper includes both quantitative and qualitative evaluations, demonstrating the performance of the framework. The use of metrics like SSIM and Hausdorff Distance is appropriate, and the visual assessments provide further evidence of the quality of the generated images.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **ODE model assumptions:** The ODE model relies on certain assumptions about tumor growth dynamics (e.g., exponential growth before RT).  These assumptions may not always hold true in vivo, and model misspecification could impact the accuracy of the predictions.  The model parameters are also fit independently per slice, which doesn't account for tumor morphology across slices.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework is relatively complex, involving multiple components (ODE model, DDIM, regressor). This complexity could make it difficult to implement and maintain.
    *   **Limited validation data:** While the framework is evaluated on a cohort of DMG patients, the number of patients (8 for the mechanistic modeling and 8 for longitudinal validation, and 29 total) is relatively small. A larger-scale validation study would be needed to confirm the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Single contrast input for DDIM:** The work relies on multiparametric MRI data, and the utility for a single contrast MRI sequence would be beneficial.
*   **Potential Impact:** The approach has the potential to influence research in several areas:
    *   **Longitudinal tumor modeling:** It provides a new way to integrate mechanistic models with generative models for predicting tumor growth over time.
    *   **Medical image synthesis:** The framework can be used to generate realistic medical images for training other machine learning models or for clinical decision support.
    *   **Rare disease research:** The data-efficient learning capabilities of the framework make it particularly valuable for research in rare diseases.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses discussed above, I assign a score of 7.  The paper presents a genuinely novel combination of mechanistic and generative models with demonstrable utility in a clinically important application. The integration is well-motivated and addresses a real-world challenge in neuro-oncology. The strengths of the approach are evident in the results presented. However, the limitations related to model assumptions, complexity, validation data, and potential difficulty in implementation temper the score. Furthermore, the reliance on fitting the ODE independently per slice, thereby losing the 3D tumor structure, negatively impacts the clinical utility to some degree.  A score of 7 reflects the significant contribution while acknowledging the areas for further refinement and validation.

Score: 7
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LoCoBench: A Benchmark for Long-Context Large Language Models in Complex Software Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09614v1)
- **Authors**: Jielin Qiu, Zuxin Liu, Zhiwei Liu, Rithesh Murthy, Jianguo Zhang, Haolin Chen, Shiyu Wang, Ming Zhu, Liangwei Yang, Juntao Tan, Zhepeng Cen, Cheng Qian, Shelby Heinecke, Weiran Yao, Silvio Savarese, Caiming Xiong, Huan Wang
- **Abstract**: The emergence of long-context language models with context windows extending to millions of tokens has created new opportunities for sophisticated code understanding and software development evaluation. We propose LoCoBench, a comprehensive benchmark specifically designed to evaluate long-context LLMs in realistic, complex software development scenarios. Unlike existing code evaluation benchmarks that focus on single-function completion or short-context tasks, LoCoBench addresses the critical evaluation gap for long-context capabilities that require understanding entire codebases, reasoning across multiple files, and maintaining architectural consistency across large-scale software systems. Our benchmark provides 8,000 evaluation scenarios systematically generated across 10 programming languages, with context lengths spanning 10K to 1M tokens, a 100x variation that enables precise assessment of long-context performance degradation in realistic software development settings. LoCoBench introduces 8 task categories that capture essential long-context capabilities: architectural understanding, cross-file refactoring, multi-session development, bug investigation, feature implementation, code comprehension, integration testing, and security analysis. Through a 5-phase pipeline, we create diverse, high-quality scenarios that challenge LLMs to reason about complex codebases at unprecedented scale. We introduce a comprehensive evaluation framework with 17 metrics across 4 dimensions, including 8 new evaluation metrics, combined in a LoCoBench Score (LCBS). Our evaluation of state-of-the-art long-context models reveals substantial performance gaps, demonstrating that long-context understanding in complex software development represents a significant unsolved challenge that demands more attention. LoCoBench is released at: https://github.com/SalesforceAIResearch/LoCoBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LoCoBench paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LoCoBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate long-context language models (LLMs) specifically in complex software engineering tasks. It addresses perceived shortcomings in existing benchmarks, particularly their limited scale, short context windows, and narrow task scopes. LoCoBench features:

*   **Large-scale, diverse dataset:** 8,000 scenarios across 10 programming languages and 36 domains, systematically varying context lengths from 10K to 1M tokens.
*   **Comprehensive task categories:** Evaluates architectural understanding, cross-file refactoring, multi-session development, bug investigation, feature implementation, code comprehension, integration testing, and security analysis.
*   **Novel evaluation metrics:**  Introduces new metrics such as Architectural Coherence Score (ACS), Dependency Traversal Accuracy (DTA), and Multi-Session Memory Retention (MMR) to assess long-context capabilities beyond functional correctness.
*   **A systematic pipeline:** A 5-phase generation process to create high-quality, realistic codebases and evaluation scenarios.

The authors evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs using LoCoBench, revealing performance gaps and highlighting the challenges of long-context understanding in complex software development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by explicitly addressing the need for benchmarks that go beyond single-function completion and short-context tasks. The emphasis on evaluating long-context capabilities in a realistic software development environment is innovative. The newly introduced metrics provide a more granular assessment of architectural understanding and contextual awareness, which are essential for evaluating LLMs in realistic software development settings. The systematic approach to code and scenario generation, coupled with the comprehensive validation process, adds rigor to the benchmark.
*   **Significance:**  The paper clearly identifies a critical gap in the evaluation of long-context LLMs, which are increasingly being applied to sophisticated code understanding and generation tasks. The findings from LoCoBench highlight areas where current LLMs struggle, such as architectural coherence and maintaining context across multiple files and development sessions. By providing a comprehensive benchmark and evaluation framework, the paper facilitates further research and development in this area. The public release of LoCoBench encourages community engagement and collaboration.
*   **Strengths:** The benchmark's scale and diversity in programming languages and domains is commendable.  The systematic approach to varying context length is crucial for assessing performance degradation. The task categories are well-chosen to capture essential software engineering skills.  The authors thoughtfully address potential biases and ensure data quality through a rigorous validation process. The comprehensive evaluation framework, combining existing metrics with novel metrics specifically designed for long-context capabilities, enables a more comprehensive assessment of model performance.
*   **Weaknesses:** While the paper describes the generation process in detail, more information on the specific algorithms used for context selection, difficulty calibration, and bias detection would be beneficial. The evaluation metrics rely on automated methods; a small-scale human evaluation to validate the correlation between automated scores and human judgment could further strengthen the paper. It would be helpful to see a more thorough ablative study on the individual impact of new metrics like ACS, DTA, and MMR. Also, although the study includes diverse languages, an analysis of language-specific performance could provide insights into model biases. Further analysis of correlation between the code metrics and the LLMs' performance in relevant tasks would be beneficial.
*   **Potential Influence:** LoCoBench is likely to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating LLMs in software engineering. The insights gained from LoCoBench will guide the development of more effective long-context models and contribute to the advancement of AI-assisted software development.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper. While the paper is not a complete paradigm shift (which would justify a 9 or 10), LoCoBench represents a significant advancement in the evaluation of LLMs for software engineering. The creation of the dataset and the proposed metrics are substantial contributions. The systematic design, large scale, diversity, and comprehensive task categories make this a valuable resource for the research community. While there are areas for improvement as outlined above, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, making it a significant and influential contribution to the field.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Bridging the Capability Gap: Joint Alignment Tuning for Harmonizing LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09629v1)
- **Authors**: Minghang Zhu, Zhengliang Shi, Zhiwei Xu, Shiguang Wu, Lingjie Wang, Pengjie Ren, Zhaochun Ren, Zhumin Chen
- **Abstract**: The advancement of large language models (LLMs) has enabled the construction of multi-agent systems to solve complex tasks by dividing responsibilities among specialized agents, such as a planning agent for subgoal generation and a grounding agent for executing tool-use actions. Most existing methods typically fine-tune these agents independently, leading to capability gaps among them with poor coordination. To address this, we propose MOAT, a Multi-Agent Joint Alignment Tuning framework that improves agents collaboration through iterative alignment. MOAT alternates between two key stages: (1) Planning Agent Alignment, which optimizes the planning agent to generate subgoal sequences that better guide the grounding agent; and (2) Grounding Agent Improving, which fine-tunes the grounding agent using diverse subgoal-action pairs generated by the agent itself to enhance its generalization capablity. Theoretical analysis proves that MOAT ensures a non-decreasing and progressively convergent training process. Experiments across six benchmarks demonstrate that MOAT outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving average improvements of 3.1% on held-in tasks and 4.4% on held-out tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Bridging the Capability Gap: Harmonizing Multi-Agent Systems via Joint Alignment Tuning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of capability gaps and coordination failures in multi-agent systems built using Large Language Models (LLMs).  It proposes MOAT (Multi-Agent Joint Alignment Tuning), a framework that iteratively aligns a planning agent (responsible for subgoal generation) and a grounding agent (responsible for executing tool-use actions). MOAT alternates between two stages: Planning Agent Alignment (optimizing the planning agent to generate subgoals that are easier for the grounding agent to understand and execute) and Grounding Agent Improving (fine-tuning the grounding agent using subgoal-action pairs generated by the planning agent). The approach uses Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to align the planning agent and a critic model to refine grounding agent training.  The authors provide theoretical analysis to demonstrate non-decreasing performance and convergence. Experiments on six benchmarks show that MOAT outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in both held-in and held-out settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of jointly aligning planning and grounding agents in a multi-agent LLM system is relatively novel. While prior work has explored multi-agent systems and agent tuning, most approaches train agents independently. The iterative alignment approach, especially the use of PPL to guide the planning agent and a critic model to refine the grounding agent, represents a meaningful contribution. The theoretical analysis, while not groundbreaking, provides a useful foundation for understanding the framework's behavior.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a practical and important problem in the development of effective LLM-based agents. Capability gaps between agents can severely limit the performance of multi-agent systems. MOAT offers a promising solution that demonstrably improves coordination and overall task-solving ability. The experimental results are compelling and span a diverse set of benchmarks. The fact that MOAT achieves significant improvements over strong baselines, including GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-4 on a specific task after being trained only on smaller 7b open-source LLMs, highlights its potential for practical use. The inclusion of ablation studies further strengthens the paper by demonstrating the importance of each component of the MOAT framework.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of capability gaps in multi-agent systems.
    *   **Novel Approach:** MOAT's iterative joint alignment is a novel and effective solution.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides theoretical analysis to support the approach.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization ability of MOAT.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies demonstrate the contribution of each component of the MOAT framework.
    *   **Detailed analysis:** The hyperparameter impact and capability gap are analyzed thoroughly.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on a Critic Model:** The grounding agent improving stage relies on a powerful critic model for action refinement. The reliance on a closed-source, very large model (GPT-4) as a critic may limit the accessibility or reproducibility of this work in a practical setting if the closed-source models are not readily available, although the results demonstrate strong results even with a smaller open-source alternative.
    *   **Complexity:**  MOAT introduces additional complexity compared to independent agent training. The iterative alignment process and the need for a critic model add to the computational overhead.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The framework is currently evaluated only on text-based tasks. The potential applicability to multimodal settings remains an open question.
    *   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:** While the overall framework is novel, techniques like DPO are already well-established, the individual contributions are not necessarily breakthrough innovations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and effective multi-agent systems. The joint alignment approach could be adopted in other multi-agent architectures and extended to address other challenges, such as communication and conflict resolution.
    The strong experimental findings and the clear articulation of the problem may encourage further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper.

*   The problem of coordinating agents with disparate skill sets is a challenge for scalable LLM agents and therefore, has **high significance** to the artificial intelligence and machine learning communities.

*   The MOAT methodology, while using well-established techniques (DPO), combines these techniques in a novel and significant way to address a well-defined problem.

*   The experiments are thorough across multiple tasks and models. The strong experimental findings **validate MOAT's effectiveness.** The inclusion of ablation studies shows a deep understanding of the framework.

*   There are some limitations, such as the need for a strong critic (partially mitigated by using an open source option), the need for parameter tuning, and evaluation only on text data. The **overall framework may be difficult to implement** in some edge cases.

Overall, the paper presents a solid contribution with a good balance of theory and experimental validation. It is a well-written and significant contribution to the field of LLM-based multi-agent systems.
